# Exceptional Lie groups 

Ichiro Yokota

## Preface

In the end of 19 century, W. Killing and E. Cartan classified the complex simple Lie algebras, called $A_{n}, B_{n}, C_{n}, D_{n}$ (classical type) and $G_{2}, F_{4}, E_{6}, E_{7}, E_{8}$ (exceptional type). These simple Lie algebras and the corresponding compact simple Lie groups have offered many subjects in mathematicians. Especially, exceptional Lie groups are very wonderful and interesting miracle in Lie group theory.

Now, in the present book, we describe simply connected compact exceptional simple Lie groups $G_{2}, F_{4}, E_{6}, E_{7}, E_{8}$, in very elementary way. The contents are given as follows. We first construct all simply connected compact exceptional Lie groups $G$ concretely. Next, we find all involutive automorphisms $\sigma$ of $G$, and determine the group structures of the fixed points subgroup $G^{\sigma}$ by $\sigma$. Note that they correspond to classification of all irreducible compact symmetric spaces $G / G^{\sigma}$ of exceptional type, and that they also correspond to classification of all non-compact exceptional simple Lie groups. Finally, we determined the group structures of the maximal subgroups of maximal rank. At any rate, we would like this book to be used in mathematics and physics.

The author thanks K. Abe, K. Mituishi, T. Miyasaka, T. Miyashita, T. Sato, O. Shukuzawa, K. Takeuchi and O. Yasukura for their advices and encouragements.

Ichiro Yokota

## Notation

$\boldsymbol{R}, \boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{R} \oplus \boldsymbol{R} e_{1}, \boldsymbol{H}=\boldsymbol{R} \oplus \boldsymbol{R} e_{1} \oplus \boldsymbol{R} e_{2} \oplus \boldsymbol{R} e_{3}$ denote the fields of real, complex, quaternion numbers, respectively.

For $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector space $V$, its complexification $\{u+i v \mid u, v \in V\}$ is denoted by $V^{C}$. The complex conjugation in $V^{C}$ is denoted by $\tau$ :

$$
\tau(u+i v)=u-i v .
$$

$\boldsymbol{R}^{C}$ is briefly denoted by $C$.
For $K$-vector space $V(K=\boldsymbol{R}, \boldsymbol{C}, C), \operatorname{Iso}_{K}(V)$ denotes all $K$-linear isomorphisms of $V$. For a $K$-linear mapping $f$ of $V, V_{f}$ denotes $\{v \in V \mid f(v)=v\}$.

For $K$-vector spaces $V, W(K=\boldsymbol{R}, C), \operatorname{Hom}_{K}(V, W)$ denotes all $K$-homomorphism $f: V \rightarrow W . \operatorname{Hom}_{K}(V, V)$ is briefly denoted by $\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(V)$.

Let $G$ be a group and $\sigma$ an automorphism of $G$. Then $G^{\sigma}$ denotes $\{g \in G \mid \sigma(g)=$ $g\}$. For $s \in G, G^{s}$ denotes $\left\{g \in G \mid s g s^{-1}=g\right\}$.

For topological spaces $X, Y, X \simeq Y$ denotes that $X$ and $Y$ are homeomorphic.
For groups $G, G^{\prime}, G \cong G^{\prime}$ denotes that $G$ and $G^{\prime}$ are isomorphic as groups. Isomorphic two groups $G, G^{\prime}$ are often identified: $G=G^{\prime}$.
$M(n, K)$ denotes all $n \times n$ matrices with entries in $K$.
$E=\operatorname{diag}(1, \cdots, 1) \in M(n, K)$ is the unit matrix.
For $A \in M(n, K),{ }^{t} A$ denotes the transposed matrix of $A$ and $A^{*}$ denotes the conjugate transposed matrix of $A: A^{*}={ }^{t} \bar{A}$.
$O(n)=\left\{\left.A \in M(n, \boldsymbol{R})\right|^{t} A A=E\right\}$ (orthogonal group),
$S O(n)=\{A \in O(n) \mid \operatorname{det} A=1\}$ (spcecial orthogonal group),
$U(n)=\left\{A \in M(n, \boldsymbol{C}) \mid A^{*} A=E\right\}$ or $\left\{A \in M(n, C) \mid \tau\left({ }^{t} A\right) A=E\right\}$ (unitary group),
$S U(n)=\{A \in U(n) \mid \operatorname{det} A=1\} \quad$ (speccial unitary group),
$\operatorname{Sp}(n)=\left\{A \in M(n, \boldsymbol{H}) \mid A^{*} A=E\right\}$ (symplectic group).
For a Lie group $G$, its Lie algebra is denoted by the correspoding small Germann letter $\mathfrak{g}$. For example, $\mathfrak{s u}(n)$ is the Lie algebra of the special unitary group $\operatorname{SU}(n)$.

Special notations

$$
\gamma, \quad \sigma, \quad \iota, \quad \kappa, \quad \lambda, \quad \mu, \quad \tau, \quad v, \quad \chi, \quad g, \quad w .
$$

## Exceptional Lie group $G_{2}$

### 1.1. Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}$

We denote the division Cayley algebra by $\mathfrak{C}$. We now explain this algebra. Consider an 8 dimensional $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector space with basis $\left\{e_{0}=1, e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}, e_{4}, e_{5}, e_{6}, e_{7}\right\}$ and define a multiplication between them as follows. In the figure below, the multiplication between $e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}$ is defined as

$$
e_{1} e_{2}=e_{3}, \quad e_{2} e_{3}=e_{1}, \quad e_{3} e_{1}=e_{2},
$$

and defined similarly on the other lines. For example, $e_{1} e_{6}=e_{7}, e_{4} e_{7}=e_{3}$ etc. We regard that $e_{2}, e_{5}, e_{7}$ are also collinear, for example, $e_{5} e_{7}=e_{2} . e_{0}=1$ is the unit of the multiplication and assume

$$
e_{i}^{2}=-1, \quad i \neq 0, \quad e_{i} e_{j}=-e_{j} e_{i}, \quad i \neq j, i \neq 0, j \neq 0,
$$

and the distributive law. Thus $\mathfrak{C}$ has a multiplication. $x 1, x \in \boldsymbol{R}$ is briefly denoted by $x$. In $\mathfrak{C}$, the conjugate $\bar{x}$, an inner product $(x, y)$, the length $|x|$ and the real part $R(x)$ are defined respectively by
![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/1deb350a-8d75-4da5-b1ec-153e0bfd7145-006.jpg?height=480&width=568&top_left_y=1376&top_left_x=821)

$$
\begin{array}{cr}
\overline{x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{7} x_{i} e_{i}}=x_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{7} x_{i} e_{i}, & \left(\sum_{i=0}^{7} x_{i} e_{i}, \sum_{i=0}^{7} y_{i} e_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{7} x_{i} y_{i} \\
|x|=\sqrt{(x, x)}, & R\left(x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{7} x_{i} e_{i}\right)=x_{0} .
\end{array}
$$

For $x \in \mathfrak{C}, x \neq 0$, we denote $\frac{\bar{x}}{|x|^{2}}$ by $x^{-1}$, then we have $x x^{-1}=x^{-1} x=1$, and $\mathfrak{C}$ satisfies all axioms of a field except the associative law $x(y z)=(x y) z$. Of course the commutative law $x y=y x$ does not hold. Since the associative law does not
hold, calculations in $\mathfrak{C}$ are complicated, however the following relations hold. (See Freudenthal [6] or Yokota [58]). For $a, b, x, y \in \mathfrak{C}$, we have

$1 \quad(x y, x y)=(x, x)(y, y), \quad|x y|=|x||y|$.
$2 \quad(a x, a y)=(a, a)(x, y)=(x a, y a)$.
$3(a x, b y)+(b x, a y)=2(a, b)(x, y)$,
$4 \quad(a x, y)=(x, \bar{a} y), \quad(x a, y)=(x, y \bar{a})$.
$5 \quad \overline{\bar{x}}=x, \quad \overline{x+y}=\bar{x}+\bar{y}, \quad \overline{x y}=\bar{y} \bar{x}$.
$6 \quad(x, y)=(y, x)=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{x} y+\bar{y} x)=\frac{1}{2}(x \bar{y}+y \bar{x}), \quad \bar{x} x=x \bar{x}=|x|^{2}$.
$7 \quad a(\bar{a} x)=(a \bar{a}) x, \quad a(x \bar{a})=(a x) \bar{a}, \quad x(a \bar{a})=(x a) \bar{a}$.
$a(a x)=(a a) x, \quad a(x a)=(a x) a, \quad x(a a)=(x a) a$,
$8 \bar{b}(a x)+\bar{a}(b x)=2(a, b) x=(x a) \bar{b}+(x b) \bar{a}$.

9 We use a notation $\{x, y, z\}=(x y) z-x(y z)$, called the associator of $x, y, z$. Then, we have

$$
\{x, y, a\}=\{y, a, x\}=\{a, x, y\}=-\{y, x, a\}=-\{x, a, y\}=-\{a, y, x\} .
$$

For example, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& (a x) y+x(y a)=a(x y)+(x y) a, \\
& (x a) y+(x y) a=x(a y)+x(y a), \\
& (a x) y+(x a) y=a(x y)+x(a y) .
\end{aligned}
$$

$10 \quad(a x)(y a)=a(x y) a \quad$ (Moufang's formula).
$11 R(x y)=R(y x), \quad R(x(y z))=R(y(z x))=R(z(x y))(=R(x y z))$.

For an orthonormal basis $\left\{1, a_{1}, a_{2}, \cdots, a_{7}\right\}$ of $\mathfrak{C}$ with respect to the inner product $(x, y)$, the following 12.1 ~ 12.3 hold.

$12.1 \quad a_{i}\left(a_{j} x\right)=-a_{j}\left(a_{i} x\right), \quad$ in particular, $\quad a_{i} a_{j}=-a_{j} a_{i}, \quad i \neq j$.
$12.2 \quad a_{i}\left(a_{i} x\right)=-x$, in particular, $\quad a_{i}{ }^{2}=-1$.
$12.3 a_{i}\left(a_{j} a_{k}\right)=a_{j}\left(a_{k} a_{i}\right)=a_{k}\left(a_{i} a_{j}\right), \quad i, j, k$ are distinct.

### 1.2. Compact exceptional Lie group $G_{2}$

Definition. The group $G_{2}$ is defined to be the automorphism group of the Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}$ :

$$
G_{2}=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) \mid \alpha(x y)=(\alpha x)(\alpha y)\right\} .
$$

Lemma 1.2.1. For $\alpha \in G_{2}$, we have

$$
(\alpha x, \alpha y)=(x, y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Proof. For $\alpha \in G_{2}$ and $x \in \mathfrak{C}$, we have

$$
\overline{\alpha x}=\alpha \bar{x} .
$$

To prove this, it is sufficient to show

$$
\alpha 1=1, \quad \overline{\alpha e_{i}}=-\alpha e_{i}, \quad i=1,2, \cdots, 7 .
$$

From $(\alpha 1)(\alpha 1)=\alpha(1 \cdot 1)=\alpha 1$ and $\alpha 1 \neq 0$, we have $\alpha 1=1$, while from $\left(\alpha e_{i}\right)\left(\alpha e_{i}\right)=$ $\alpha\left(e_{i} e_{i}\right)=\alpha(-1)=-\alpha 1=-1$ for $i \neq 0$, we get $\overline{\alpha e_{i}}=-\alpha e_{i}$. Now,

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\alpha x, \alpha y) & =\frac{1}{2}((\alpha x)(\overline{\alpha y})+(\alpha y)(\overline{\alpha x}))=\frac{1}{2}((\alpha x)(\alpha \bar{y})+(\alpha y)(\alpha \bar{x})) \\
& =\alpha\left(\frac{1}{2}(x \bar{y}+y \bar{x})\right)=\alpha((x, y))=(x, y) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Theorem 1.2.2. $G_{2}$ is a compact Lie group.
Proof. $G_{2}$ is a compact Lie group as a closed subgroup of the orthogonal group

$$
O(8)=O(\mathfrak{C})=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) \mid(\alpha x, \alpha y)=(x, y)\right\} .
$$

Remark. Since $\alpha 1=1$ for $\alpha \in G_{2}, G_{2}$ is a subgroup of the orthogonal group $O(7)=\{\alpha \in O(\mathfrak{C}) \mid \alpha 1=1\}$, that is, $G_{2} \subset O(7)$.

### 1.3. Outer automorphisms of Lie algebra $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$

In order to study the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ of the group $G_{2}$, we consider the Lie algebra

$$
\mathfrak{D}_{4}=\mathfrak{s o}(8)=\mathfrak{s o}(\mathfrak{C})=\left\{D \in \operatorname{Hom}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) \mid(D x, y)+(x, D y)=0\right\}
$$

of the Lie group $S O(8)$.
We define $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear mappings $G_{i j}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}, i, j=0,1, \cdots, 7, i \neq j$ satisfying

$$
G_{i j} e_{j}=e_{i}, \quad G_{i j} e_{i}=-e_{j}, \quad G_{i j} e_{k}=0, k \neq i, j .
$$

Then $G_{i j} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ and $\left\{G_{i j} \mid 0 \leq i<j \leq 7\right\}$ forms an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$. Furthermore we define $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear mappings $F_{i j}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}, i, j=0,1, \cdots, 7, i \neq j$ by

$$
F_{i j} x=\frac{1}{2} e_{i}\left(\bar{e}_{j} x\right), \quad x \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Lemma 1.3.1. For $i, j=0,1, \cdots, 7, i \neq j$, we have $F_{i j} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$, and when $i<j$, $F_{i j}$ is expressed in terms of $G_{i j}$ as follows.

$$
\left\{\begin{array} { l } 
{ 2 F _ { 0 1 } = G _ { 0 1 } + G _ { 2 3 } + G _ { 4 5 } + G _ { 6 7 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 2 3 } = G _ { 0 1 } + G _ { 2 3 } - G _ { 4 5 } - G _ { 6 7 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 4 5 } = G _ { 0 1 } - G _ { 2 3 } + G _ { 4 5 } - G _ { 6 7 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 6 7 } = G _ { 0 1 } - G _ { 2 3 } - G _ { 4 5 } + G _ { 6 7 } , }
\end{array} \left\{\begin{array}{l}
2 F_{02}=G_{02}-G_{13}-G_{46}+G_{57} \\
2 F_{13}=-G_{02}+G_{13}-G_{46}+G_{57} \\
2 F_{46}=-G_{02}-G_{13}+G_{46}+G_{57} \\
2 F_{57}=G_{02}+G_{13}+G_{46}+G_{57},
\end{array}\right.\right.
$$

$$
\begin{gathered}
\left\{\begin{array} { l } 
{ 2 F _ { 0 3 } = G _ { 0 3 } + G _ { 1 2 } + G _ { 4 7 } + G _ { 5 6 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 1 2 } = G _ { 0 3 } + G _ { 1 2 } - G _ { 4 7 } - G _ { 5 6 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 4 7 } = G _ { 0 3 } - G _ { 1 2 } + G _ { 4 7 } - G _ { 5 6 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 5 6 } = G _ { 0 3 } - G _ { 1 2 } - G _ { 4 7 } + G _ { 5 6 } , }
\end{array} \left\{\begin{array}{l}
2 F_{04}=G_{04}-G_{15}+G_{26}-G_{37} \\
2 F_{15}=-G_{04}+G_{15}+G_{26}-G_{37} \\
2 F_{26}=G_{04}+G_{15}+G_{26}+G_{37} \\
2 F_{37}=-G_{04}-G_{15}+G_{26}+G_{37},
\end{array}\right.\right. \\
\left\{\begin{array} { l } 
{ 2 F _ { 0 5 } = G _ { 0 5 } + G _ { 1 4 } - G _ { 2 7 } - G _ { 3 6 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 1 4 } = G _ { 0 5 } + G _ { 1 4 } + G _ { 2 7 } + G _ { 3 6 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 2 7 } = - G _ { 0 5 } + G _ { 1 4 } + G _ { 2 7 } - G _ { 3 6 } } \\
{ 2 F _ { 3 6 } = - G _ { 0 5 } + G _ { 1 4 } - G _ { 2 7 } + G _ { 3 6 } , }
\end{array} \left\{\begin{array}{l}
2 F_{06}=G_{06}-G_{17}-G_{24}+G_{35} \\
2 F_{17}=-G_{06}+G_{17}-G_{24}+G_{35} \\
2 F_{24}=-G_{06}-G_{17}+G_{24}+G_{35} \\
2 F_{35}=G_{06}+G_{17}+G_{24}+G_{35},
\end{array}\right.\right. \\
\left\{\begin{array}{l}
2 F_{07}=G_{07}+G_{16}+G_{25}+G_{34} \\
2 F_{16}=G_{07}+G_{16}-G_{25}-G_{34} \\
2 F_{25}=G_{07}-G_{16}+G_{25}-G_{34} \\
2 F_{34}=G_{07}-G_{16}-G_{25}+G_{34} .
\end{array}\right.
\end{gathered}
$$

In particular, $\left\{F_{i j} \mid 0 \leq i<j \leq 7\right\}$ forms an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$.
Definition. We define $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear mappings $\kappa, \pi, \nu: \mathfrak{D}_{4} \rightarrow \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\kappa D) x & =\overline{D \bar{x}}, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C}, \\
\pi\left(G_{i j}\right) & =F_{i j}, \quad i, j=0,1, \cdots, 7, i \neq j, \\
\nu & =\pi \kappa .
\end{aligned}
$$

Lemma 1.3.2. The mappings $\kappa, \pi, \nu$ are automorphisms of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$ :

$$
\kappa, \pi, \nu \in \operatorname{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{4}\right) .
$$

Proof. Since $\kappa^{2}=1, \kappa$ is an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear isomorphism of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$. We have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& {\left[\kappa D_{1}, \kappa D_{2}\right] x=\left(\kappa D_{1}\right)\left(\kappa D_{2} x\right)-\left(\kappa D_{2}\right)\left(\kappa D_{1} x\right)=\overline{D_{1}\left(\overline{\kappa D_{2} x}\right)}-\overline{D_{2}\left(\overline{\kappa D_{1} x}\right)}} \\
& \quad=\overline{D_{1} D_{2} \bar{x}}-\overline{D_{2} D_{1} \bar{x}}=\kappa\left(D_{1} D_{2}-D_{2} D_{1}\right) x=\kappa\left[D_{1}, D_{2}\right] x, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C}
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\kappa \in \operatorname{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{4}\right)$. Since $\pi$ maps the $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis $\left\{G_{i j} \mid 0 \leq i<j \leq 7\right\}$ to the $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis $\left\{F_{i j} \mid 0 \leq i<j \leq 7\right\}$ of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}, \pi$ is an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear isomorphism. To show that $\pi$ is an automorphism of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$, it is sufficient to show that

$$
\left[\pi G_{i j}, \pi G_{k l}\right]=\pi\left[G_{i j}, G_{k l}\right],
$$

which in turn would follow from the relations

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
{\left[F_{i j}, F_{j k}\right]=F_{i k},} & i, j, k \text { are distinct } \\
{\left[F_{i j}, F_{k l}\right]=0,} & i, j, k, l \text { are distinct. }
\end{array}
$$

In the following calculations, let $i, j, k, l$ be all distinct and $i, j, k, l \neq 0$. For $x \in \mathfrak{C}$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
{\left[F_{i 0}, F_{0 k}\right] x } & =\left(F_{i 0} F_{0 k}-F_{0 k} F_{i 0}\right) x=-\frac{1}{4} e_{i}\left(e_{k} x\right)+\frac{1}{4} e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right) \\
& =\frac{1}{2} e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right)=F_{i k} x,
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
{\left[F_{i 0}, F_{k l}\right] x } & =\left(F_{i 0} F_{k l}-F_{k l} F_{i 0}\right) x=\frac{1}{4} e_{i}\left(e_{l}\left(e_{k} x\right)\right)-\frac{1}{4} e_{l}\left(e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right)\right) \\
& =-\frac{1}{4} e_{l}\left(e_{i}\left(e_{k} x\right)\right)+\frac{1}{4} e_{l}\left(e_{i}\left(e_{k} x\right)\right)=0, \\
{\left[F_{i j}, F_{j k}\right] x } & =\left(F_{i j} F_{j k}-F_{j k} F_{i j}\right) x=\frac{1}{4} e_{j}\left(e_{i}\left(e_{k}\left(e_{j} x\right)\right)\right)-\frac{1}{4} e_{k}\left(e_{j}\left(e_{j}\left(e_{i} x\right)\right)\right) \\
& =\frac{1}{4} e_{i}\left(e_{k}\left(e_{j}\left(e_{j} x\right)\right)\right)+\frac{1}{4} e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right)=-\frac{1}{4} e_{i}\left(e_{k} x\right)+\frac{1}{4} e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right) \\
& =\frac{1}{2} e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right)=F_{i k} x, \\
{\left[F_{i j}, F_{k l}\right] x } & =\left(F_{i j} F_{k l}-F_{k l} F_{i j}\right) x=\frac{1}{4} e_{j}\left(e_{i}\left(e_{l}\left(e_{k} x\right)\right)\right)-\frac{1}{4} e_{l}\left(e_{k}\left(e_{j}\left(e_{i} x\right)\right)\right) \\
& =\frac{1}{4} e_{j}\left(e_{k}\left(e_{i}\left(e_{l} x\right)\right)\right)-\frac{1}{4} e_{j}\left(e_{l}\left(e_{k}\left(e_{i} x\right)\right)\right)=\cdots=0 .
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\pi \in \operatorname{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{4}\right)$. Finally, since $\nu=\pi \kappa$, we have $\nu \in \operatorname{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{4}\right)$.
Definition. For $a \in \mathfrak{C}$, we define $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear mappings $L_{a}, R_{a}, T_{a}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ respectively by

$$
L_{a} x=a x, \quad R_{a} x=x a, \quad T_{a} x=a x+x a=\left(L_{a}+R_{a}\right) x, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Hereafter, we denote by $\mathfrak{C}_{0}$ the subset $\{a \in \mathfrak{C} \mid \bar{a}=-a\}$ of $\mathfrak{C}$.
Lemma 1.3.3. For $a \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$, we have

$$
L_{a}, R_{a}, T_{a} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4} .
$$

$$
\kappa L_{a}=-R_{a}, \quad \kappa R_{a}=-L_{a}, \quad \kappa T_{a}=-T_{a} .
$$

$$
\pi L_{a}=T_{a}, \quad \pi R_{a}=-R_{a}, \quad \pi T_{a}=L_{a} .
$$

$$
\nu L_{a}=R_{a}, \quad \nu R_{a}=-T_{a}, \quad \nu T_{a}=-L_{a} .
$$

Proof. (1) $\left(L_{a} x, y\right)=(a x, y)=(x, \bar{a} y)=-(x, a y)=-\left(x, L_{a} y\right), x, y \in \mathfrak{C}$. Hence $L_{a} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$. Similarly, $R_{a} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ and $T_{a}=L_{a}+R_{a} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$.
(2) $\left(\kappa L_{a}\right) x=\overline{L_{a} \bar{x}}=\overline{a \bar{x}}=x \bar{a}=-x a=-R_{a} x, x \in \mathfrak{C}$. Hence $\kappa L_{a}=-R_{a}$. The others can be similarly obtained.
(3) It is sufficient to show that these relations hold for $a=e_{i}, i=1, \cdots, 7$. We have

$$
L_{e_{i}}=2 F_{i 0}, \quad T_{e_{i}}=2 G_{i 0} .
$$

Indeed,

$$
\begin{aligned}
& L_{e_{i}} x=e_{i} x=2 F_{i 0} x, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C}, \\
& T_{e_{i}} x=e_{i} x+x e_{i}= \begin{cases}2 e_{i}, & x=1 \\
-2, & x=e_{i} \\
0, & x=e_{j}, \quad j \neq 0, i .\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
$$

It follows that

$$
\pi L_{e_{i}}=\pi\left(2 F_{i 0}\right)=-2 \pi F_{0 i}=-2 G_{0 i}(\text { Lemma 1.3.1 })=2 G_{i 0}=T_{e_{i}},
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \pi T_{e_{i}}=\pi\left(2 G_{i 0}\right)=2 F_{i 0}=L_{e_{i}}, \\
& \pi R_{a}=\pi\left(T_{a}-L_{a}\right)=L_{a}-T_{a}=-R_{a} .
\end{aligned}
$$

(4) follows immediately from (2) and (3), since $\nu=\pi \kappa$.

Lemma 1.3.4. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$ is generated by $\left\{L_{a} \mid a \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}\right\}$ (by taking at most one time the Lie bracket [ , ]), that is, any $D \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ is expressed by

$$
D=L_{a}+\sum_{i}\left[L_{b_{i}}, L_{c_{i}}\right], \quad a, b_{i}, c_{i} \in \mathfrak{C}_{0} .
$$

Proof. Let $\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}$ be the Lie subalgebra of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$ generated by $\left\{L_{a} \mid a \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}\right\}$. Since $L_{e_{i}}=2 F_{i 0}$ and $\left[L_{e_{i}}, L_{e_{j}}\right]=4\left[F_{i 0}, F_{j 0}\right]=-4 F_{i j}, i \neq 0, j \neq 0, i \neq j$, we see that $\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}$ contains $F_{i j}, i, j=0,1, \cdots, 7, i \neq j$. Since $\left\{F_{i j}, i<j\right\}$ is an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$ (Lemma 1.3.1), $\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}$ coincides with $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$.

Theorem 1.3.5. In the automorphism group $\operatorname{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{4}\right)$ of $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$, the subgroup $\mathfrak{S}_{3}$ generated by $\kappa$ and $\pi$ is isomorphic to the symmetric group $S_{3}$ of degree 3. Furthermore, $\kappa, \pi, \nu$ have the following relations.

$$
\kappa^{2}=1, \quad \pi^{2}=1, \quad \nu^{3}=1, \quad \nu=\pi \kappa .
$$

Proof. Since $\left\{L_{a} \mid a \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}\right\}$ generates $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$ (Lemma 1.3.4), it is sufficient to check $\kappa^{2}=1, \pi^{2}=1, \nu^{3}=1$ etc. for $L_{a}$. However, these relations follow from Lemma 1.3.3.(2),(3),(4). The mapping $f: \mathfrak{S}_{3} \rightarrow S_{3}$ defined by the correspondence

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
1 \rightarrow\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 2 & 3 \\
1 & 2 & 3
\end{array}\right), \quad \kappa \rightarrow\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 2 & 3 \\
2 & 1 & 3
\end{array}\right), \quad \pi \rightarrow\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 2 & 3 \\
3 & 2 & 1
\end{array}\right), \\
\nu \rightarrow\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 2 & 3 \\
2 & 3 & 1
\end{array}\right), \quad \nu^{2} \rightarrow\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 2 & 3 \\
3 & 1 & 2
\end{array}\right), \quad \nu \pi \rightarrow\left(\begin{array}{lll}
1 & 2 & 3 \\
1 & 3 & 2
\end{array}\right)
\end{array}
$$

gives an isomorphism as groups.
Theorem 1.3.6. (Principle of infinitesimal triality in $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$ ). For any $D_{1} \in$ $\mathfrak{D}_{4}$, there exist $D_{2}, D_{3} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ such that

$$
\left(D_{1} x\right) y+x\left(D_{2} y\right)=D_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Also such $D_{2}, D_{3}$ are uniquely determined for $D_{1}$ and we have

$$
D_{2}=\nu D_{1}, \quad D_{3}=\pi D_{1} .
$$

Proof. If $a \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$, then $L_{a}, R_{a}, T_{a} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ (Lemma 1.3.3.(1)) and the equality $(a x) y+x(y a)=a(x y)+(x y) a$ implies that

$$
\left(L_{a} x\right) y+x\left(R_{a} y\right)=T_{a}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Similarly, for $b \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$, we have

$$
\left(L_{b} x\right) y+x\left(R_{b} y\right)=T_{b}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Applying $T_{a}$ on (ii) and using (i), we have

$$
\left(L_{a} L_{b} x\right) y+\left(L_{b} x\right)\left(R_{a} y\right)+\left(L_{a} x\right)\left(R_{b} y\right)+x\left(R_{a} R_{b} y\right)=T_{a} T_{b}(x y) .
$$

Exchanging $a$ for $b$, and subtracting from the above, we get

$$
\left(\left[L_{a}, L_{b}\right] x\right) y+x\left(\left[R_{a}, R_{b}\right] y\right)=\left[T_{a}, T_{b}\right](x y) .
$$

Since $D_{1} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ is expressed as

$$
D_{1}=L_{a}+\sum\left[L_{b}, L_{c}\right], \quad a, b, c \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}
$$

(Lemma 1.3.4), putting $D_{2}=R_{a}+\sum\left[R_{b}, R_{c}\right], D_{3}=T_{a}+\sum\left[T_{b}, T_{c}\right]$ and using (i), (iii), we obtain

$$
\left(D_{1} x\right) y+x\left(D_{2} y\right)=D_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Next, we shall show that $D_{2}$ and $D_{3}$ are determined uniquely for $D_{1}$. To prove this, it is sufficient to show for $D_{1}=0$ that $D_{2}=D_{3}=0$. Now, suppose

$$
x\left(D_{2} y\right)=D_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Putting $x=1$, we have $D_{2} y=D_{3} y$, so that $D_{2}=D_{3}(=D)$. Therefore

$$
x(D y)=D(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Putting $D 1=p$, we have $2(p, 1)=(p, 1)+(1, p)=(D 1,1)+(1, D 1)=0$, which implies $p \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$. Furthermore, putting $y=1$ in (iv), we have $x p=D x$, so (iv) becomes

$$
x(y p)=(x y) p, \quad \text { for all } x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

We therefore see that $p \in \boldsymbol{R}$, so that $p=0$ since $p \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$. Hence $D x=x p=0$, and so $D=0$. This proves the uniqueness. Finally, if we express $D_{1} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ as $D_{1}=L_{a}+\sum\left[L_{b}, L_{c}\right], a, b, c \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$, then $D_{2}=R_{a}+\sum\left[R_{b}, R_{c}\right], D_{3}=T_{a}+\sum\left[T_{b}, T_{c}\right]$ from the arguments above and the uniqueness. Hence we have $D_{2}=\nu D_{1}, D_{3}=\pi D_{1}$ (Lemma 1.3.3).

Lemma 1.3.7. For $D_{1}, D_{2}, D_{3} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$, the relation

$$
\left(D_{1} x\right) y+x\left(D_{2} y\right)=\left(\kappa D_{3}\right)(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C}
$$

implies

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
\left(D_{2} x\right) y+x\left(D_{3} y\right)=\left(\kappa D_{1}\right)(x y), & x, y \in \mathfrak{C}, \\
\left(D_{3} x\right) y+x\left(D_{1} y\right)=\left(\kappa D_{2}\right)(x y), & x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
\end{array}
$$

Proof. For $D_{2} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$, there exist $D_{3}{ }^{\prime}, D_{1}{ }^{\prime} \in \mathfrak{D}_{4}$ such that

$$
\left(D_{2} x\right) y+x\left(D_{3}^{\prime} y\right)=\left(\kappa D_{1}^{\prime}\right)(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C},
$$

and $D_{3}{ }^{\prime}=\nu D_{2}, \kappa D_{1}{ }^{\prime}=\pi D_{2}$ (Theorem 1.3.6). The assumption of the lemma is $D_{2}=\nu D_{1}, \kappa D_{3}=\pi D_{1}$ (Theorem 1.3.6). Hence, using Theorem 1.3.5, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& D_{3}^{\prime}=\nu D_{2}=\nu \nu D_{1}=\nu^{-1} D_{1}=\kappa \pi D_{1}=\kappa \kappa D_{3}=D_{3} \\
& D_{1}^{\prime}=\kappa \pi D_{2}=\nu^{-1} D_{2}=D_{1}
\end{aligned}
$$

1.4. Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ of $G_{2}$

Theorem 1.4.1. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ of the Lie group $G_{2}$ is given by

$$
\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\left\{D \in \operatorname{Hom}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) \mid D(x y)=(D x) y+x(D y)\right\} .
$$

Proof. If $D \in \operatorname{Hom}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C})$ satisfies $(\exp t D)(x y)=((\exp t D) x)((\exp t D) y), t \in \boldsymbol{R}$, then by differentiating with respect to $t$ and putting $t=0$, we get $D(x y)=(D x) y+$ $x(D y)$. Conversely, if $D \in \operatorname{Hom}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C})$ satisfies $D(x y)=(D x) y+x(D y)$, then it is not difficult to verify that $\alpha=\exp t D$ satisfies $\alpha(x y)=(\alpha x)(\alpha y)$.

In order to study the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$, we need the following Lie algebra $\mathfrak{b}_{3}=\mathfrak{s o}(7)$ of $S O(7)$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak{b}_{3} & =\left\{D \in \mathfrak{D}_{4} \mid D 1=0\right\} \\
& =\left\{D \in \mathfrak{D}_{4} \mid \kappa D=D\right\}=\left\{D \in \mathfrak{D}_{4} \mid \nu D=\pi D\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Lemma 1.4.2. $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ is a Lie subalgebra of $\mathfrak{b}_{3}$. Moreover we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak{g}_{2} & =\left\{D \in \mathfrak{D}_{4} \mid \nu D=D, \pi D=D\right\} \\
& =\left\{D \in \mathfrak{D}_{4} \mid \lambda D=D, \lambda \in \mathfrak{S}_{3}\right\} \\
& =\left\{D \in \mathfrak{b}_{3} \mid \pi D=D\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Proof. Let $D \in \mathfrak{g}_{2}$. Putting $x=y=1$ in $D(x y)=(D x) y+x(D y)$, we get $D 1=0$. We next show that

$$
D x \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

If $i \neq 0$, then $\left(D e_{i}\right) e_{i}+e_{i}\left(D e_{i}\right)=D\left(e_{i} e_{i}\right)=D(-1)=0$, and so $D e_{i} \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$. Together with $D 1=0$, we have $D x \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}, x \in \mathfrak{C}$. Now, note that

$$
x y+y x=-(x \bar{y}+y \bar{x})=-2(x, y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C}_{0} .
$$

Applying $D$ on the relation above, we have $(D x) y+x(D y)+(D y) x+y(D x)=0$. Since $D x, D y \in \mathfrak{C}_{0}$, we get

$$
(D x, y)+(x, D y)=0, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Hence $\mathfrak{g}_{2} \subset \mathfrak{b}_{3}$. The relation in the lemma follows easily from Theorem 1.3.5 and Theorem 1.3.6.

Theorem 1.4.3. Any element of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ is expressed by the sum of elements of the following seven types.

$$
\begin{array}{cll}
\lambda G_{23}+\mu G_{45}+\nu G_{67}, & -\lambda G_{13}-\mu G_{46}+\nu G_{57}, & \\
\lambda G_{12}+\mu G_{47}+\nu G_{56}, & -\lambda G_{15}+\mu G_{26}-\nu G_{37}, & \lambda, \mu, \nu \in \boldsymbol{R} \\
\lambda G_{14}-\mu G_{27}-\nu G_{36}, & -\lambda G_{17}-\mu G_{24}+\nu G_{35}, & \lambda+\mu+\nu=0 \\
& \lambda G_{16}+\mu G_{25}+\nu G_{34}
\end{array}
$$

Conversely, the above seven elements belong to $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$. In particular, the dimension of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ is 14:

$$
\operatorname{dim} \mathfrak{g}_{2}=14 .
$$

Proof. Let $D \in \mathfrak{g}_{2}$. Since $D \in \mathfrak{b}_{3}$ (Lemma 1.4.2), $D=\sum_{0<i<j} \lambda_{i j} G_{i j}, \lambda_{i j} \in \boldsymbol{R}$. The condition $\pi D=D$ (Lemma 1.4.2) implies that

$$
\sum_{0<i<j} \lambda_{i j} F_{i j}=\sum_{0<i<j} \lambda_{i j} G_{i j} .
$$

Applying the above element on $1 \in \mathfrak{C}$, we have

$$
\sum_{0<i<j} \lambda_{i j} e_{j} e_{i}=0 .
$$

Replacing $e_{j} e_{i}$ by $e_{k}$ and comparing the coefficient of each $e_{1}, e_{2}, \cdots, e_{7}$, we have

$$
\begin{gathered}
\lambda_{23}+\lambda_{45}+\lambda_{67}=0, \quad-\lambda_{13}-\lambda_{46}+\lambda_{57}=0, \\
\lambda_{12}+\lambda_{47}+\lambda_{56}=0, \quad-\lambda_{15}+\lambda_{26}-\lambda_{37}=0, \\
\lambda_{14}-\lambda_{27}-\lambda_{36}=0, \quad-\lambda_{17}-\lambda_{24}+\lambda_{35}=0, \\
\lambda_{16}+\lambda_{25}+\lambda_{34}=0,
\end{gathered}
$$

from which the first result follows. Conversely, any of these seven elements $D$ obviously belongs to $\mathfrak{b}_{3}$ and the condition $\pi D=D$ (Lemma 1.4.2) is verified from the table of Lemma 1.3.1.

### 1.5. Lie subalgebra $\mathfrak{s u}(3)$ of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$

The Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}$ naturally contains the field $\boldsymbol{C}$ of complex numbers as

$$
\boldsymbol{C}=\left\{x_{0}+x_{1} e_{1} \mid x_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}\right\} .
$$

Any element $x \in \mathfrak{C}$ is expressed by

$$
\begin{aligned}
x & =x_{0}+x_{1} e_{1}+x_{2} e_{2}+x_{3} e_{3}+x_{4} e_{4}+x_{5} e_{5}+x_{6} e_{6}+x_{7} e_{7} \quad\left(x_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}\right) \\
& =\left(x_{0}+x_{1} e_{1}\right)+\left(x_{2}+x_{3} e_{1}\right) e_{2}+\left(x_{4}+x_{5} e_{1}\right) e_{4}+\left(x_{6}+x_{7} e_{1}\right) e_{6},
\end{aligned}
$$

that is,

$$
x=a+m_{1} e_{2}+m_{2} e_{4}+m_{3} e_{6}, \quad a, m_{i} \in \boldsymbol{C} .
$$

We associate such element $x$ of $\mathfrak{C}$ with the element of $\boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}$

$$
a+\left(\begin{array}{l}
m_{1} \\
m_{2} \\
m_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

In $\boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}$, we define a multiplication, an inner product ( , ) and a conjugation - respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
(a+\boldsymbol{m})(b+\boldsymbol{n}) & =(a b-\langle\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n}\rangle)+(a \boldsymbol{n}+\bar{b} \boldsymbol{m}-\overline{\boldsymbol{m} \times \boldsymbol{n}}), \\
(a+\boldsymbol{m}, b+\boldsymbol{n}) & =(a, b)+(\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n}), \\
\overline{a+\boldsymbol{m}} & =\bar{a}-\boldsymbol{m},
\end{aligned}
$$

where the real valued symmetric inner product $(\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n})$, the Hermitian inner product $\langle\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n}\rangle$ and the exterior product $\boldsymbol{m} \times \boldsymbol{n}$ are usually defined respectively by

$$
\begin{gathered}
(\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{m}^{*} \boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}^{*} \boldsymbol{m}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left(m_{i}, n_{i}\right), \quad\langle\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{3} m_{i} \bar{n}_{i} \\
\boldsymbol{m} \times \boldsymbol{n}=\left(\begin{array}{c}
m_{2} n_{3}-n_{2} m_{3} \\
m_{3} n_{1}-n_{3} m_{1} \\
m_{1} n_{2}-n_{1} m_{2}
\end{array}\right)
\end{gathered}
$$

for $\boldsymbol{m}=\left(\begin{array}{c}m_{1} \\ m_{2} \\ m_{3}\end{array}\right), \boldsymbol{n}=\left(\begin{array}{c}n_{1} \\ n_{2} \\ n_{3}\end{array}\right) \in \boldsymbol{C}^{3}$. Since these operations correspond to their respective operations in $\mathfrak{C}$, hereafter, we identify $\boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}$ with $\mathfrak{C}$, that is,

$$
\boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C} .
$$

We shall study the following subalgebra $\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ :

$$
\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}=\left\{D \in \mathfrak{g}_{2} \mid D e_{1}=0\right\} .
$$

Theorem 1.5.1. $\quad\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}} \cong \mathfrak{s u}(3)$.
Proof. We define a mapping $\varphi_{*}: \mathfrak{s u}(3)=\left\{D \in M(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \mid D^{*}=-D, \operatorname{tr}(D)=\right.$ $0\} \rightarrow\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ by

$$
\varphi_{*}(D)(a+\boldsymbol{m})=D \boldsymbol{m}, \quad a+\boldsymbol{m} \in \boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C} .
$$

We first prove that $\varphi_{*}(D) \in\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$. For elements

$$
\begin{array}{llll}
e_{1}\left(E_{11}-E_{22}\right), & e_{1}\left(E_{22}-E_{33}\right), & E_{12}-E_{21}, & e_{1}\left(E_{12}+E_{21}\right), \\
E_{13}-E_{31}, & e_{1}\left(E_{13}+E_{31}\right), & E_{23}-E_{32}, & e_{1}\left(E_{23}+E_{32}\right)
\end{array}
$$

of an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$ (where $E_{k l} \in M(3, \boldsymbol{R})$ is the matrix with the $(k, l)$-entry is 1 and otherwise are 0), we have

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
\varphi_{*}\left(e_{1}\left(E_{11}-E_{22}\right)\right)=-G_{23}+G_{45}, & \varphi_{*}\left(e_{1}\left(E_{22}-E_{33}\right)\right)=-G_{45}+G_{67}, \\
\varphi_{*}\left(E_{12}-E_{21}\right)=G_{24}+G_{35}, & \varphi_{*}\left(e_{1}\left(E_{12}+E_{21}\right)\right)=-G_{25}+G_{34}, \\
\varphi_{*}\left(E_{13}-E_{31}\right)=G_{26}+G_{37}, & \varphi_{*}\left(e_{1}\left(E_{13}+E_{31}\right)\right)=-G_{27}+G_{36}, \\
\varphi_{*}\left(E_{23}-E_{32}\right)=G_{46}+G_{57}, & \varphi_{*}\left(e_{1}\left(E_{23}+E_{32}\right)\right)=-G_{47}+G_{56} .
\end{array}
$$

Hence $\varphi_{*}(D) \subset \mathfrak{g}_{2}$ (Theorem 1.4.3). Clearly $\varphi_{*}(D) e_{1}=0$, so that $\varphi_{*}(D) \subset\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$. Obviously $\varphi_{*}: \mathfrak{s u}(3) \rightarrow \mathfrak{g}_{2}$ is a homomorphism as Lie algebras and is injective, so that we identify $\mathfrak{s u}(3)$ and $\varphi_{*}(\mathfrak{s u}(3)$. We set

$$
\begin{aligned}
S_{1} & =2 G_{12}-G_{47}-G_{56}, \quad S_{2}=2 G_{13}-G_{46}+G_{57}, \\
S_{3} & =2 G_{14}+G_{27}+G_{36}, \quad S_{4}=2 G_{15}+G_{26}-G_{37}, \\
S_{5} & =2 G_{16}-G_{25}-G_{34}, \quad S_{6}=2 G_{17}-G_{24}+G_{35},
\end{aligned}
$$

and let $\mathfrak{S}$ be the $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector subspace of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ spanned by $S_{1}, \cdots, S_{6}$. Then we have the following decomposition of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$.

$$
\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3) \oplus \mathfrak{S} .
$$

Now, we shall show that $\varphi_{*}: \mathfrak{s u}(3) \rightarrow\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ is onto. Let $B \in\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$. Denote

$$
B=D+\sum_{i=1}^{6} x_{i} S_{i}, \quad D \in \mathfrak{s u}(3), x_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}
$$

From the condition $B e_{1}=0$, we have

$$
-2 x_{1} e_{2}-2 x_{2} e_{3}-2 x_{3} e_{4}-2 x_{4} e_{5}-2 x_{5} e_{6}-2 x_{6} e_{7}=0 .
$$

Hence $x_{1}=\cdots=x_{6}=0$, so that $B=D \in \mathfrak{s u}(3)$. Thus the proof of Theorem 1.5.1 is completed.

### 1.6. Simplicity of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$

Let $\mathfrak{C}^{C}=\left\{x_{1}+i x_{2} \mid x_{1}, x_{2} \in \mathfrak{C}\right\}$ be the complexification of the Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}$. In the same manner as in $\mathfrak{C}$, we can also define in $\mathfrak{C}^{C}$ the multiplication $x y$, the inner product $(x, y)$ such that they satisfy properties $1 \sim 12.3$ of Section 1.1 (except some formulas about the length $|x|$ ). $\mathfrak{C}^{C}$ is called the complex Cayley algebra. $\mathfrak{C}^{C}$ has two complex conjugations, namely,

$$
\overline{x_{1}+i x_{2}}=\bar{x}_{1}+i \bar{x}_{2}, \quad \tau\left(x_{1}+i x_{2}\right)=x_{1}-i x_{2}, \quad x_{i} \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

The complex conjugation $\tau$ is a complex-conjugate linear transformation of $\mathfrak{C}^{C}$ and satisfies

$$
\tau(x y)=(\tau x)(\tau y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C}^{C} .
$$

For a while, in the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{s u}(3) \subset \mathfrak{g}_{2}$, we use the following notations.

$$
\begin{gathered}
H_{1}=-G_{23}+G_{45}, \quad H_{2}=-G_{45}+G_{67} \\
L_{12}=G_{24}+G_{35}, \quad L_{21}=-G_{25}+G_{34}, \quad L_{13}=G_{26}+G_{37}, \\
L_{21}=-G_{27}+G_{36}, \quad L_{23}=G_{46}+G_{57}, \quad L_{32}=-G_{47}+G_{56} .
\end{gathered}
$$

Lemma 1.6.1. The Lie brackets $[D, S], D \in \mathfrak{s u}(3), S \in \mathfrak{S}$ are given as follows.

|  | $S_{1}$ | $S_{2}$ | $S_{3}$ | $S_{4}$ | $S_{5}$ | $S_{6}$ |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $H_{1}$ | $S_{2}$ | $-S_{1}$ | $-S_{4}$ | $S_{3}$ | 0 | 0 |
| $H_{2}$ | 0 | 0 | $S_{4}$ | $-S_{3}$ | $-S_{6}$ | $S_{5}$ |
| $L_{12}$ | $-S_{3}$ | $-S_{4}$ | $S_{1}$ | $S_{2}$ | 0 | 0 |
| $L_{21}$ | $S_{4}$ | $-S_{3}$ | $S_{2}$ | $-S_{1}$ | 0 | 0 |
| $L_{13}$ | $-S_{5}$ | $-S_{6}$ | 0 | 0 | $S_{1}$ | $S_{2}$ |
| $L_{31}$ | $S_{6}$ | $-S_{5}$ | 0 | 0 | $S_{2}$ | $-S_{1}$ |
| $L_{23}$ | 0 | 0 | $-S_{5}$ | $-S_{6}$ | $S_{3}$ | $S_{4}$ |
| $L_{32}$ | 0 | 0 | $S_{6}$ | $-S_{5}$ | $S_{4}$ | $-S_{3}$ |

Lemma 1.6.2. $\mathfrak{S}$ is a $\mathfrak{s u}(3)$-irreducible $\boldsymbol{R}$-module, and hence we have

$$
[\mathfrak{s u}(3), \mathfrak{S}]=\mathfrak{S} .
$$

Proof. Evidently $\mathfrak{S}$ is a $\mathfrak{s u}(3)$ - $\boldsymbol{R}$-module. Let $W$ be a non-zero $\mathfrak{s u}(3)$-invariant $\boldsymbol{R}$-snbmodule of $\mathfrak{S}$. If $W$ contains some $S_{k}$, then, from the table of Lemma 1.6.1, we can see that $W$ contains all $S_{k}, k=1,2, \cdots, 6$, and hence $W=\mathfrak{S}$. Now, let $S=\sum_{k=1}^{6} x_{k} S_{k}, x_{k} \in \boldsymbol{R}$ be a non-zero element of $W$ and assume that $x_{1} \neq 0$ (without the loss of generality). Applying $H_{1}$ on $S$, we have $x_{1} S_{2}-x_{2} S_{1}-x_{3} S_{4}+x_{4} S_{3} \in W$. Next, applying $L_{13}$ and $L_{31}$ on it, we have

$$
-x_{1} S_{6}+x_{2} S_{5} \in W \quad \cdots(\mathrm{i}) \quad \text { and } \quad-x_{1} S_{5}-x_{2} S_{6} \in W
$$

Taking (ii) $\times x_{2}$ - (i) $\times x_{1}$, we have $\left(x_{1}{ }^{2}+x_{2}{ }^{2}\right) S_{5} \in W$. Since $x_{1}{ }^{2}+x_{2}{ }^{2} \neq 0$, we have $S_{5} \in W$ and so $W=\mathfrak{S}$. Consequently the irreducibility of $\mathfrak{S}$ is proved. Finally, since $[\mathfrak{s u}(3), \mathfrak{S}]$ is a $\mathfrak{s u}(3)$-invariant $\boldsymbol{R}$-submodle of $\mathfrak{S}$, from the irreducibility of $\mathfrak{S}$, we have $[\mathfrak{s u}(3), \mathfrak{S}]=\mathfrak{S}$.

Theorem 1.6.3. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ is simple and so $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ is also simple.
Proof. We shall prove that $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ is simple, because the proof of that $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ is simple is the same as the case of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$. We use the decomposition of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$

$$
\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{s u}(3) \oplus \mathfrak{S} \quad \text { (Theorem 1.5.1). }
$$

Let $p: \mathfrak{g}_{2} \rightarrow \mathfrak{s u}(3)$ and $q: \mathfrak{g}_{2} \rightarrow \mathfrak{S}$ be projections of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{s u}(3) \oplus \mathfrak{S}$. Now, let $\mathfrak{a}$ be a non-zero ideal of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$. Then $p(\mathfrak{a})$ is an ideal of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$. Indeed, if $D \in p(\mathfrak{a})$, then there exists $S \in \mathfrak{S}$ such that $D+S \in \mathfrak{a}$. For any $D^{\prime} \in \mathfrak{s u}(3)$, we have

$$
\mathfrak{a} \ni\left[D^{\prime}, D+S\right]=\left[D^{\prime}, D\right]+\left[D^{\prime}, S\right], \quad\left[D^{\prime}, S\right] \in \mathfrak{S}
$$

(Lemma 1.6.1), hence $\left[D^{\prime}, D\right] \in p(\mathfrak{a})$.
We show that either $\mathfrak{s u}(3) \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$ or $\mathfrak{S} \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$. Assume that $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3) \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$ and $\mathfrak{S} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$. The mapping $p \mid \mathfrak{a}: \mathfrak{a} \rightarrow \mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$ is injective because $\mathfrak{S} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$. Since $p(\mathfrak{a})$ is a non-zero ideal of $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$ and $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$ is simple, we have $p(\mathfrak{a})=\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$. Hence $\operatorname{dim} \mathfrak{a}=\operatorname{dim} p(\mathfrak{a})=\operatorname{dim} \mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)=8$. On the other hand, since $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3) \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$, $q \mid \mathfrak{a}: \mathfrak{a} \rightarrow \mathfrak{S}$ is also injective, we have $\operatorname{dim} \mathfrak{a} \leq \operatorname{dim} \mathfrak{S}=6$. This leads to a contradiction.

We now consider the following two cases.
(1) Case $\mathfrak{s u}(3) \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$. From the simplicity of $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$, we have $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3) \cap \mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$, hence $\mathfrak{a} \supset \mathfrak{s u}(3)$. On the other hand, we have

$$
\mathfrak{a} \supset[\mathfrak{a}, \mathfrak{S}] \supset[\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3), \mathfrak{S}]=\mathfrak{S} \text { (Lemma 1.6.1). }
$$

Hence $\mathfrak{a} \supset \mathfrak{s u}(3) \oplus \mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{g}_{2}$.
(2) Case $\mathfrak{S} \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$. Choose a non-zero element $S \in \mathfrak{S} \cap \mathfrak{a} \subset \mathfrak{a}$. Under the actions of $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)$, we can see that $S_{1} \in \mathfrak{a}$ (Lemma 1.6.1). Hence $0 \neq 4 H_{1}+2 H_{2}=\left[S_{1}, S_{2}\right] \in \mathfrak{a}$. So this case can be reduced to the case (1). Thus we have $\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{g}_{2}$, which proves the simplicity of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$.

### 1.7. Killing form of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$

Lemma 1.7.1. In $\mathfrak{s u}(3) \subset \mathfrak{g}_{2}$, the Lie brackets between $H_{1}$ and $L_{i j}, L_{j i}$ of Section 1.6 are given by

$$
\begin{array}{lll}
{\left[H_{1}, L_{12}\right]=2 L_{21},} & {\left[H_{1}, L_{21}\right]=-2 L_{12},} & {\left[H_{1}, L_{13}\right]=L_{31},} \\
{\left[H_{1}, L_{31}\right]=-L_{13},} & {\left[H_{1}, L_{23}\right]=-L_{32},} & {\left[H_{1}, L_{32}\right]=L_{23} .}
\end{array}
$$

Theorem 1.7.2. The killing form $B_{2}$ of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ is given by

$$
B_{2}\left(D_{1}, D_{2}\right)=4 \operatorname{tr}\left(D_{1} D_{2}\right), \quad D_{i} \in \mathfrak{g}_{2}^{C} .
$$

Proof. Since $\operatorname{tr}\left(D_{1} D_{2}\right)$ is a $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$-adjoint invariant bilinear form of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ and $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ is simple (Theorem 1.6.3), there exists $k \in C$ such that

$$
B_{2}\left(D_{1}, D_{2}\right)=k \operatorname{tr}\left(D_{1} D_{2}\right) .
$$

To determine this $k$, let $D_{1}=D_{2}=H_{1}$. Then from

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
{\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, L_{12}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1}, 2 L_{21}\right]=-4 L_{12},} & {\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, L_{21}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1},-2 L_{12}\right]=-4 L_{21},} \\
{\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, L_{13}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1}, L_{31}\right]=-L_{13},} & {\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, L_{31}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1},-L_{13}\right]=-L_{31},} \\
{\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, L_{23}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1},-L_{32}\right]=-L_{23},} & {\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, L_{32}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1}, L_{23}\right]=-L_{32},}
\end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
{\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, S_{1}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1}, S_{2}\right]=-S_{1},} & {\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, S_{2}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1},-S_{1}\right]=-S_{2},} \\
{\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, S_{3}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1},-S_{4}\right]=-S_{3},} & {\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, S_{4}\right]\right]=\left[H_{1}, S_{3}\right]=-S_{4},} \\
{\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, S_{5}\right]\right]=0,\left[H_{1},\left[H_{1}, S_{6}\right]\right]=0} &
\end{array}
$$

(Lemma 1.6.1), we have

$$
B_{2}\left(H_{1}, H_{1}\right)=\operatorname{tr}\left(\left(\operatorname{ad} H_{1}\right)^{2}\right)=(-4) \times 2+(-1) \times 8=-16 .
$$

On the other hand,

$$
\begin{aligned}
H_{1} H_{1} e_{2} & =H_{1} e_{3}=-e_{2}, \quad
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\operatorname{tr}\left(H_{1} H_{1}\right)=(-1) \times 4=-4$. Therefore $k=4$.

### 1.8. Roots of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$

We recall the $C$-Lie isomorphism $f_{*}: \mathfrak{s l}(3, C) \rightarrow \mathfrak{s u}(3)^{C}$ and the embedding $\varphi_{*}$ : $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{u}(3)^{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$,

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
f_{*}(A)=\varepsilon A-\bar{\varepsilon}^{t} A, & \varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+i e_{1}\right) \\
\varphi_{*}(D)(a+\boldsymbol{m})=D \boldsymbol{m}, & a+\boldsymbol{m} \in \boldsymbol{C}^{C} \oplus\left(\boldsymbol{C}^{3}\right)^{C}=\mathfrak{C}^{C}
\end{array}
$$

and we regard $\mathfrak{s l}(3, C)$ as a subalgebra of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ under the composition of $f_{*}$ and $\varphi_{*}$. Further we know that the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{s l}(3, C)$ has roots $\pm\left(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l}\right), 1 \leq k<l \leq 3$ relative to the Cartan subalgebra

$$
\mathfrak{h}=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\lambda_{1} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \lambda_{2} & 0 \\
0 & 0 & \lambda_{3}
\end{array}\right) \right\rvert\, \lambda_{i} \in \boldsymbol{C}, \lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}=0\right\}
$$

of $\mathfrak{s l}(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, and $E_{k l}$ is a root vector associated with the root $\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l}$.
Theorem 1.8.1. The rank of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ is 2. The roots of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ relative to some Cartan subalgebra of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ are given by

$$
\pm\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\right), \quad \pm\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}\right), \quad \pm\left(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}\right), \quad \pm \lambda_{1}, \quad \pm \lambda_{2} \quad \pm \lambda_{3}
$$

with $\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}=0$.
Proof. $\quad \mathfrak{h}=\left\{-i \lambda_{1} G_{23}-i \lambda_{2} G_{45}-i \lambda_{3} G_{67} \in \mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C} \mid \lambda_{k} \in C\right\} \subset \mathfrak{s l}(3, C) \subset \mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ is an abelian subalgebra of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ (it will be a Cartan subalgebra of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ ). The roots of $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{l}(3, C)$ is also roots of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$, so we have the table of roots and associated root vectors as follows.

$$
\begin{array}{ll} 
\pm\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\right) & : \pm\left(G_{24}+G_{35}\right)+i\left(-G_{25}+G_{34}\right), \\
\pm\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}\right) & : \pm\left(G_{26}+G_{37}\right)+i\left(-G_{27}+G_{36}\right), \\
\pm\left(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}\right) & : \pm\left(G_{46}+G_{57}\right)+i\left(-G_{47}+G_{56}\right) .
\end{array}
$$

The remainder roots and associated root vectors are found as follows.

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \pm \lambda_{1}:\left(2 G_{12}-G_{47}-G_{56}\right) \pm i\left(2 G_{13}-G_{46}+G_{57}\right), \\
& \pm \lambda_{2}:\left(2 G_{14}+G_{27}+G_{36}\right) \pm i\left(2 G_{15}+G_{26}-G_{37}\right), \\
& \pm \lambda_{3}:\left(2 G_{16}-G_{25}-G_{34}\right) \pm i\left(2 G_{17}-G_{24}+G_{35}\right) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Theorem 1.8.2. In the root system of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ of Theorem 1.8.1,

$$
\alpha_{1}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}, \quad \alpha_{2}=\lambda_{2}
$$

is a fundamental root system of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ and

$$
\mu=2 \alpha_{1}+3 \alpha_{3}
$$

is the highest root. The Dynkin diagram and the extended Dynkin diagram of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ are respectively given by
![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/1deb350a-8d75-4da5-b1ec-153e0bfd7145-020.jpg?height=70&width=145&top_left_y=1140&top_left_x=796)
![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/1deb350a-8d75-4da5-b1ec-153e0bfd7145-020.jpg?height=100&width=240&top_left_y=1113&top_left_x=1093)

Proof. All positive roots of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$ are expressed by

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2} & =\alpha_{1}, & \lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3} & =2 \alpha_{1}+3 \alpha_{2}, & \lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3} & =\alpha_{1}+3 \alpha_{2}, \\
\lambda_{1} & =\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}, & \lambda_{2} & =\alpha_{2}, & & =\lambda_{3}+2 \alpha_{2}
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\Pi=\left\{\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}\right\}$ is a fundamental root system of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$. The real part of $\mathfrak{h}_{\boldsymbol{R}}$ of $\mathfrak{h}$ is

$$
\mathfrak{h}_{\boldsymbol{R}}=\left\{-i \lambda_{1} G_{23}-i \lambda_{2} G_{45}-i \lambda_{3} G_{67} \mid \lambda_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}, \lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}=0\right\}
$$

and the Killing form $B_{2}$ on $\mathfrak{h}_{\boldsymbol{R}}$ is given by

$$
B_{2}\left(H, H^{\prime}\right)=8 \sum_{k=1}^{3} \lambda_{k} \lambda_{k}^{\prime},
$$

for $H=-i \lambda_{1} G_{23}-i \lambda_{2} G_{45}-i \lambda_{3} G_{67}, H^{\prime}=-i \lambda_{1}{ }^{\prime} G_{23}-i \lambda_{2}{ }^{\prime} G_{45}-i \lambda_{3}{ }^{\prime} G_{67} \in \mathfrak{h}_{\boldsymbol{R}}$ (Theorem 1.7.2). Now, the canonical element $H_{\alpha_{i}} \in \mathfrak{h}_{\boldsymbol{R}}$ associated with $\alpha_{i}\left(B_{2}\left(H_{\alpha}, H\right)=\right.$ $\alpha(H), H \in \mathfrak{h}_{\boldsymbol{R}}$ ) are determined as follows.

$$
H_{\alpha_{1}}=-\frac{1}{8} i G_{23}+\frac{1}{8} i G_{45}, \quad H_{\alpha_{2}}=\frac{1}{24} i G_{23}-\frac{1}{12} i G_{45}+\frac{1}{24} i G_{67} .
$$

Hence we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{1}\right)=B_{2}\left(H_{\alpha_{1}}, H_{\alpha_{1}}\right)=8 \frac{1}{8} \frac{1}{8}(1+1)=\frac{1}{4} \\
& \left(\alpha_{2}, \alpha_{2}\right)=B_{2}\left(H_{\alpha_{2}}, H_{\alpha_{2}}\right)=8 \frac{1}{24} \frac{1}{24}(1+4+1)=\frac{1}{12} \\
& \left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}\right)=B_{2}\left(H_{\alpha_{1}}, H_{\alpha_{2}}\right)=8 \frac{1}{8} \frac{1}{24}(-1-2)=-\frac{1}{8} \\
& (-\mu,-\mu)=\frac{1}{4}, \quad\left(-\mu, \alpha_{1}\right)=-\frac{1}{8}, \quad\left(-\mu, \alpha_{2}\right)=0
\end{aligned}
$$

Using them, we can draw the Dynkin diagram and the extended Dynkin diagram of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$.

According to Borel-Siebenthal theory (Borel and Siebenthal [4]), the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$ has two subalgebras as maximal subalgebras with the maximal rank 2.
(1) One is a subalgebra of type $C_{1} \oplus C_{1}$ which is obtained as the fixed points by an involution $\gamma$ of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$.
(2) The other is a subalgebra of type $A_{2}$ which is obtained as the fixed points by an automorphism $w$ of order 3 of $\mathfrak{g}_{2}$.

These subalgebras will be realized in the group $G_{2}$ in Theorem 1.10.1 and Theorem 1.9.4, respectively.

### 1.9. Automorphism $w$ of order 3 and subgroup $S U(3)$ of $G_{2}$

We shall study the following subgroup $\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ of $G_{2}$ :

$$
\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}=\left\{\alpha \in G_{2} \mid \alpha e_{1}=e_{1}\right\} .
$$

Theorem 1.9.1. $\quad\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}} \cong S U(3)$.
Proof (cf. Theorem 1.5.1). We define a mapping $\varphi: S U(3) \rightarrow\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ by

$$
\varphi(A)(a+\boldsymbol{m})=a+A \boldsymbol{m}, \quad a+\boldsymbol{m} \in \boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C} .
$$

We first prove that $\varphi(A) \in\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$. For $\alpha=\varphi(A), A \in S U(3)$ and $x=a+\boldsymbol{m}, y=$ $b+\boldsymbol{n} \in \boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C}$, using that if $A \in S U(3)$ then $\widetilde{A}$ (which is the adjoint matrix of $A)=A^{-1}=A^{*}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\alpha x)(\alpha y) & =(a+A \boldsymbol{m})(b+A \boldsymbol{n}) \\
& =(a b-\langle A \boldsymbol{m}, A \boldsymbol{n}\rangle)+(a A \boldsymbol{n}+\bar{b} A \boldsymbol{m}-\overline{A \boldsymbol{m} \times A \boldsymbol{n}}) \\
& =\left(a b-\left\langle\boldsymbol{m}, A^{*} A \boldsymbol{n}\right\rangle\right)+(a A \boldsymbol{n}+\bar{b} A \boldsymbol{m}-\bar{t} \widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{m} \times \boldsymbol{n})) \\
& =(a b-\langle\boldsymbol{m}, \boldsymbol{n}\rangle)+A(a \boldsymbol{n}+\bar{b} \boldsymbol{m}-\overline{\boldsymbol{m} \times \boldsymbol{n}}) \\
& =\varphi(A)((a+\boldsymbol{m})(b+\boldsymbol{n}))=\alpha(x y)
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\varphi(A) \in G_{2}$. Clearly $\varphi(A) e_{1}=e_{1}$, so that $\varphi(A) \in\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$. Evidently $\varphi$ is a homomorphism. We show that $\varphi$ is onto. Let $\alpha \in\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$. Note that $\alpha$ induces a $\boldsymbol{C}$-linear transformation of $\boldsymbol{C}^{3}$. Now let

$$
\alpha e_{2}=\boldsymbol{a}_{1}, \quad \alpha e_{4}=\boldsymbol{a}_{2}, \quad \alpha e_{6}=\boldsymbol{a}_{3}
$$

and construct a matrix $A=\left(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}, \boldsymbol{a}_{2}, \boldsymbol{a}_{3}\right) \in M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$. From $\left(\alpha e_{2}\right)\left(\alpha e_{4}\right)=\alpha\left(e_{2} e_{4}\right)=$ $-\alpha e_{6}$, we have $\boldsymbol{a}_{1} \boldsymbol{a}_{2}=-\boldsymbol{a}_{3}$, namely, $-\left\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{1}, \boldsymbol{a}_{2}\right\rangle-\overline{\boldsymbol{a}_{1} \times \boldsymbol{a}_{2}}=-\boldsymbol{a}_{3}$, hence we have

$$
\left\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{1}, \boldsymbol{a}_{2}\right\rangle=0, \quad \boldsymbol{a}_{3}=\overline{\boldsymbol{a}_{1} \times \boldsymbol{a}_{2}} .
$$

Similarly we have $\left\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{2}, \boldsymbol{a}_{3}\right\rangle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{3}, \boldsymbol{a}_{1}\right\rangle=0$. Moerover from $\left(\alpha e_{k}\right)\left(\alpha e_{k}\right)=\alpha\left(e_{k} e_{k}\right)=$ $\alpha(-1)=-1$, we have $\left\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{k}, \boldsymbol{a}_{k}\right\rangle=1$, hence $A \in U(3)$. Finally $\operatorname{det} A=\left(\boldsymbol{a}_{3}, \boldsymbol{a}_{1} \times\right.$ $\left.\boldsymbol{a}_{2}\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{a}_{3}, \overline{\boldsymbol{a}}_{3}\right)=\left\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{3}, \boldsymbol{a}_{3}\right\rangle=1$ (where $(\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b})$ is the ordinary inner product in $\boldsymbol{C}^{3}$ : $\left.(\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b})={ }^{t} \boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{b}\right)$. Hence we have $A \in S U(3)$ and $\varphi(A)=\alpha$, which shows that $\varphi$ is onto. $\operatorname{Ker} \varphi=\{E\}$ is easily obtained. Thus we have the isomorphism $\operatorname{SU}(3) \cong\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$.

Theorem 1.9.2. $G_{2} / S U(3) \simeq S^{6}$.
Proof. $S^{6}=\{a \in \mathfrak{C}|\bar{a}=-a,|a|=1\}$ is a 6 dimensional sphere. Since the group $G_{2}$ is a subgroup of $O(7)=\{\alpha \in O(\mathfrak{C}) \mid \alpha 1=1\}$ (Remark of Theorem 1.2.2), $G_{2}$ acts on $S^{6}$. We shall show that this action is transitive. To prove this, it is sufficient to show that any element $a \in S^{6}$ can be transformed to $e_{1} \in S^{6}$ by some $\alpha \in G_{2}$. Now, for $a_{1} \in S^{6}$, choose any element $a_{2} \in S^{6}$ such that $\left(a_{1}, a_{2}\right)=0$. Let

$$
a_{3}=a_{1} a_{2} .
$$

Then $a_{3} \in S^{6}$ and $a_{3}$ satisfies $\left(a_{1}, a_{3}\right)=\left(a_{2}, a_{3}\right)=0$. Choose any element $a_{4} \in S^{6}$ such that $\left(a_{1}, a_{4}\right)=\left(a_{2}, a_{4}\right)=\left(a_{3}, a_{4}\right)=0$. Let

$$
a_{5}=a_{1} a_{4}, \quad a_{6}=a_{4} a_{2}, \quad a_{7}=a_{3} a_{4} .
$$

Then the set $\left\{a_{0}=1, a_{1}, a_{2}, \cdots, a_{7}\right\}$ is an orthonormal $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\mathfrak{C}$. Indeed, $\left|a_{i}\right|=$ $1,0 \leq i \leq 7$ are trivial, and we need to verify that $\left(a_{i}, a_{j}\right)=0, i \neq j$. However this can be checked by direct calculations such as

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left(a_{4}, a_{7}\right)=\left(a_{4}, a_{3} a_{4}\right)=\left(1, a_{3}\right)\left(a_{4}, a_{4}\right)=0, \\
& \left(a_{1}, a_{6}\right)=\left(a_{1}, a_{4} a_{2}\right)=-\left(a_{1} a_{2}, a_{4}\right)=-\left(a_{3}, a_{4}\right)=0, \\
& \left(a_{3}, a_{6}\right)=\left(a_{3}, a_{4} a_{2}\right)=-\left(a_{3} a_{2}, a_{4}\right)=\left(a_{1}, a_{4}\right)=0, \text { etc. }
\end{aligned}
$$

Now, since $\left\{e_{0}=1, e_{1}, e_{2}, \cdots, e_{7}\right\}$ and $\left\{a_{0}=1, a_{1}, a_{2}, \cdots, a_{7}\right\}$ are both orthonormal $\boldsymbol{R}$-bases, the $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear isomorphism $\alpha: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ satisfying

$$
\alpha e_{i}=a_{i}, \quad i=0,1, \cdots, 7
$$

belongs to $O(7)$ : $\alpha \in O(7)$. Moreover we claim that $\alpha \in G_{2}$, that is, $\alpha$ satisfies

$$
\alpha(x y)=(\alpha x)(\alpha y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

To show this, it is sufficient to note that

$$
\alpha\left(e_{i} e_{j}\right)=\left(\alpha e_{i}\right)\left(\alpha e_{j}\right), \quad i, j=0,1, \cdots, 7
$$

which can be also checked by direct calculations such as

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(\alpha e_{4}\right)\left(\alpha e_{7}\right) & =a_{4} a_{7}=a_{4}\left(a_{3} a_{4}\right)=-a_{4}\left(a_{4} a_{3}\right)=a_{3}=\alpha e_{3}=\alpha\left(e_{4} e_{7}\right), \\
\left(\alpha e_{1}\right)\left(\alpha e_{6}\right) & =a_{1} a_{6}=a_{1}\left(a_{4} a_{2}\right)=-a_{4}\left(a_{1} a_{2}\right)=-a_{4} a_{3}=a_{3} a_{4}=a_{7} \\
& =\alpha e_{7}=\alpha\left(e_{1} e_{6}\right), \\
\left(\alpha e_{3}\right)\left(\alpha e_{6}\right) & =a_{3} a_{6}=\left(a_{1} a_{2}\right)\left(a_{4} a_{2}\right)=-\left(a_{2} a_{1}\right)\left(a_{4} a_{2}\right)=-a_{2}\left(a_{1} a_{4}\right) a_{2} \\
& =-a_{2} a_{5} a_{2}=a_{2} a_{2} a_{5}=-a_{5}=-\alpha e_{5}=\alpha\left(e_{3} e_{6}\right), \text { etc. }
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\alpha \in G_{2}$ and $\alpha e_{1}=a_{1}$, and so $\alpha^{-1} a_{1}=e_{1}$. This shows the transitivity. The isotropy subgroup $\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ of $G_{2}$ at $e_{1}$ is $S U(3)$ (Theorem 1.9.1). Thus we have the homeomorphism $G_{2} / S U(3) \simeq S^{6}$.

Since $S^{6}$ and $S U(3)$ are both simply connected, from $G_{2} / S U(3) \simeq S^{6}$ (Theorem 1.9.2), we see that $G_{2}$ is also simply connected. Hence we have the following theorem.

Theorem 1.9.3. $G_{2}=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) \mid \alpha(x y)=(\alpha x)(\alpha y)\right\}$ is a simply connected compact simple Lie group.

Remark 1. Since $G_{2}$ is connected, $G_{2}$ is contained in $S O(7)=\{\alpha \in O(7) \mid \operatorname{det} \alpha=$ $1\}: G_{2} \subset S O(7)$.

Remark 2. Since we know that the dimension of the group $G_{2}$ as $\operatorname{dim} G_{2}=$ $\operatorname{dim} \mathfrak{g}_{2}=14$ (Theorem 1.4.3), $G_{2} / S U(3) \simeq S^{6}$ is proved as follows. The group $G_{2}$ acts on $S^{6}$. The isotropy subgroup $\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ of $G_{2}$ at $e_{1}$ is $\operatorname{SU}(3)$ (Theorem 1.9.1) and $\operatorname{dim}\left(G_{2} /\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}\right)=\operatorname{dim} G_{2}-\operatorname{dim} S U(3)=14-8=6=\operatorname{dim} S^{6}$. Therefore we have $G_{2} / S U(3) \simeq S^{6}$.

Using the mapping $\varphi: S U(3) \rightarrow G_{2}$, we define a mapping $w: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ by

$$
w=\varphi\left(\operatorname{diag}\left(\omega_{1}, \omega_{1}, \omega_{1}\right)\right)
$$

where $\omega_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} e_{1} \in \boldsymbol{C} \subset \mathfrak{C}$. This $w$ is defined as

$$
w(a+\boldsymbol{m})=a+\omega_{1} \boldsymbol{m}, \quad a+\boldsymbol{m} \in \boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C} .
$$

Then $w \in G_{2}$ and $w^{3}=1$.
We shall study the following subgroup $\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$ of $G_{2}$ :

$$
\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}=\left\{\alpha \in G_{2} \mid w \alpha=\alpha w\right\} .
$$

Theorem 1.9.4. $\quad\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}=\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}} \cong S U(3)$.
Proof. Recall the mapping $\varphi: S U(3) \rightarrow G_{2}$ of Theorem 1.9.1. We first show that $\varphi(S U(3)) \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$. Indeed, for $A \in S U(3)$ and $a+\boldsymbol{m} \in \boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
w \varphi(A)(a+\boldsymbol{m}) & =w(a+A \boldsymbol{m})=a+\omega_{1} A \boldsymbol{m} \\
& =a+A \omega_{1} \boldsymbol{m}=\varphi(A) w(a+\boldsymbol{m}) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $w \varphi(A)=\varphi(A) w$, so that $\varphi(A) \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$. Conversely, let $\alpha \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$. We consider the $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector subspace $\mathfrak{C}_{w}=\{x \in \mathfrak{C} \mid w x=x\}$ of $\mathfrak{C}$, then $\mathfrak{C}_{w}=\boldsymbol{C}$. Since $\alpha$ satisfies $w \alpha=\alpha w, \mathfrak{C}_{w}$ is invariant under $\alpha$. Since the restriction of $\alpha$ to $\mathfrak{C}_{w}$ induces an automorphism of $\boldsymbol{C}$, we have

$$
\alpha e_{1}=e_{1} \quad \text { or } \quad \alpha e_{1}=-e_{1} .
$$

In the latter case, consider the mapping $\gamma_{1}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ defined by $\gamma_{1}(a+\boldsymbol{m})=\bar{a}+\overline{\boldsymbol{m}}$. Then we have $\gamma_{1} \in G_{2}$ and $\gamma_{1} e_{1}=-e_{1}$. Let $\beta=\gamma_{1} \alpha$. Since $\beta e_{1}=e_{1}$, we have $\beta \in S U(3) \subset\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$ (Theorem 1.9.1) $\subset\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$. Therefore, $\gamma_{1}=\beta \alpha^{-1} \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$, which is a contradiction. Indeed,

$$
\omega_{1} \overline{\boldsymbol{m}}=w\left(\gamma_{1} \boldsymbol{m}\right)=\gamma_{1}(w \boldsymbol{m})=\overline{\omega_{1} \boldsymbol{m}}=\overline{\omega_{1}} \overline{\boldsymbol{m}} \quad \text { for all } \boldsymbol{m} \in \boldsymbol{C}^{3},
$$

which is false. Therefore $\alpha e_{1}=e_{1}$, and so $\alpha \in S U(3)$ (Theorem 1.9.1). Thus we have $\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}=\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}$.

### 1.10. Involution $\gamma$ and subgroup $(S p(1) \times S p(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ of $G_{2}$

The Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}$ naturally contains the field $\boldsymbol{H}$ of quaternions as

$$
\boldsymbol{H}=\left\{x_{0}+x_{1} e_{1}+x_{2} e_{2}+x_{3} e_{3} \mid x_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}\right\} .
$$

Any element $x \in \mathfrak{C}$ is expressed by

$$
\begin{aligned}
x & =x_{0}+x_{1} e_{1}+x_{2} e_{2}+x_{3} e_{3}+x_{4} e_{4}+x_{5} e_{5}+x_{6} e_{6}+x_{7} e_{7} \quad\left(x_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}\right) \\
& =\left(x_{0}+x_{1} e_{1}+x_{2} e_{2}+x_{3} e_{3}\right)+\left(x_{4}+x_{5} e_{1}-x_{6} e_{2}+x_{7} e_{3}\right) e_{4},
\end{aligned}
$$

that is,

$$
x=m+a e_{4}, \quad m, a \in \boldsymbol{H} .
$$

In $\boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}$, we define a multiplication, an inner product ( , ), a conjugation - and an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear transformation $\gamma$ respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(m+a e_{4}\right)\left(n+b e_{4}\right) & =(m n-\bar{b} a)+(a \bar{n}+b m) e_{4}, \\
\left(m+a e_{4}, n+b e_{4}\right) & =(m, n)+(a, b), \\
\overline{m+a e_{4}} & =\bar{m}-a e_{4}, \\
\gamma\left(m+a e_{4}\right) & =m-a e_{4} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Since these operations correspond to their respective operations in $\mathfrak{C}$, hereafter, we identify $\boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}$ with $\mathfrak{C}$, that is,

$$
\boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}=\mathfrak{C} .
$$

We shall study the following subgroup $\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$ of $G_{2}$ :

$$
\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}=\left\{\alpha \in G_{2} \mid \gamma \alpha=\alpha \gamma\right\} .
$$

Theorem 1.10.1. $\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma} \cong(S p(1) \times S p(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}, \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}=\{(1,1),(-1,-1)\}$.
Proof. We define a mapping $\varphi: \operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(1) \rightarrow\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$ by

$$
\varphi(p, q)\left(m+a e_{4}\right)=q m \bar{q}+(p a \bar{q}) e_{4}, \quad m+a e_{4} \in \boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}=\mathfrak{C} .
$$

We first show that $\varphi(p, q) \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$. For $\alpha=\varphi(p, q), p, q \in S p(1)$ and $x=m+a e_{4}$, $y=n+b e_{4} \in \boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}=\mathfrak{C}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\alpha x)(\alpha y) & =\left(q m \bar{q}+(p a \bar{q}) e_{4}\right)\left(q n \bar{q}+(p b \bar{q}) e_{4}\right) \\
& =((q m \bar{q})(q n \bar{q})-(\overline{p b \bar{q}})(p a \bar{q}))+((p a \bar{q})(\overline{q n \bar{q}})+(p b \bar{q})(q m \bar{q})) e_{4} \\
& =q(m n-\bar{b} a) \bar{q}+(p(a \bar{n}+b m) \bar{q}) e_{4} \\
& =\varphi(p, q)\left(\left(m+a e_{4}\right)\left(n+b e_{4}\right)\right)=\alpha(x y)
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence $\varphi(p, q) \in G_{2}$. Clearly $\gamma \varphi(p, q)=\varphi(p, q) \gamma$, so that $\varphi(p, q) \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$. Evidently $\varphi$ is a homomorphism. We shall show that $\varphi$ is onto. Let $\alpha \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$. Since $\alpha$ satisfies $\gamma \alpha=\alpha \gamma, \mathfrak{C}_{\gamma}=\{x \in \mathfrak{C} \mid \gamma x=x\}=\boldsymbol{H}$ is invariant under $\alpha$, so that the restriction of $\alpha$ to $\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma}$ induces an automorphism of $\boldsymbol{H}$. Hence there exists $q \in \operatorname{Sp}(1)$ satisfying

$$
\alpha m=q m \bar{q}, \quad m \in \boldsymbol{H}
$$

(Proposition 108 of Yokota [58]). By putting $\beta=\varphi(1, q)^{-1} \alpha$, we have $\beta \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$ and $\beta \mid \boldsymbol{H}=1$. Therfore $\beta$ induces a transformation of $\mathfrak{C}_{-\gamma}=\{x \in \mathfrak{C} \mid \gamma x=-x\}=\boldsymbol{H} e_{4}$. By putting $\beta e_{4}=p e_{4}$, where $p \in \boldsymbol{H}$, we have $|p|=\left|p e_{4}\right|=\left|\beta e_{4}\right|=\left|e_{4}\right|=1$, which implies $p \in \operatorname{Sp}(1)$. From

$$
\beta\left(m+a e_{4}\right)=\beta m+(\beta a)\left(\beta e_{4}\right)=m+a\left(p e_{4}\right)=m+(p a) e_{4}=\varphi(p, 1)\left(m+a e_{4}\right),
$$

we have $\beta=\varphi(p, 1)$. Therefore we have

$$
\alpha=\varphi(1, q) \beta=\varphi(1, q) \varphi(p, 1)=\varphi(p, q), \quad(p, q) \in \operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(1),
$$

which shows that $\varphi$ is onto. $\operatorname{Ker} \varphi=\{(1,1),(-1,-1)\}=\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ is easily obtained. Thus we have the isomorphism $(S p(1) \times S p(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \cong\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$.

Remark 1. $(S p(1) \times S p(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \cong S O(4)$.
Indeed, a mapping $f: \operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(1) \rightarrow \operatorname{SO}(4)=\operatorname{SO}(\boldsymbol{H})$ defined by

$$
f(p, q) x=p x \bar{q}, \quad x \in \boldsymbol{H}
$$

induces the isomorphism $(S p(1) \times S p(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \cong S O(4)$ as groups.
Remark 2. Since $\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$ is connected as the fixed points subgroup by an automorphism $\gamma$ of the simply connected group $G_{2}$, the fact that $\varphi: \operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(1) \rightarrow\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$ is onto can be proved as follows. The elements

$$
\begin{array}{cc}
2 G_{12}-G_{47}-G_{56}, & -G_{47}+G_{56}, \\
2 G_{13}-G_{46}-G_{57}, & G_{46}+G_{57}, \\
2 G_{23}-G_{45}-G_{67}, & -G_{45}+G_{67}
\end{array}
$$

forms an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$. So $\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)^{\gamma}=6=3+3=\operatorname{dim}(\mathfrak{s p}(1) \oplus \mathfrak{s p}(1))$. Hence $\varphi$ is onto.
1.11. Center $z\left(G_{2}\right)$ of $G_{2}$

Theorem 1.11.1. The center $z\left(G_{2}\right)$ of the group $G_{2}$ is trivial:

$$
z\left(G_{2}\right)=\{1\} .
$$

Proof. Let $\alpha \in z\left(G_{2}\right)$. From the commutativity with $\gamma: \gamma \alpha=\alpha \gamma$, we have $\alpha \in S O(4)$ (Remark 1 of Theorem 1.10.1) and so $\alpha \in z(S O(4)$ ). Since the center $z(S O(4))$ of $S O(4)$ is the group of order 2, we have

$$
\alpha=1 \quad \text { or } \quad \alpha=\gamma .
$$

However $\gamma \notin z\left(G_{2}\right)$ (Theorem 1.10.1). Hence $\alpha=1$.
1.12. Complex exceptional Lie group $G_{2}{ }^{C}$

Definition. The group $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is defined to be the automorphism group of the complex Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}^{C}$ :

$$
G_{2}{ }^{C}=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right) \mid \alpha(x y)=(\alpha x)(\alpha y)\right\} .
$$

Lemma 1.12.1. For $\alpha \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
(\alpha x, \alpha y)=(x, y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C}^{C} .
$$

Proof. The equality $\alpha x=(\alpha 1)(\alpha x)$ holds for all $x \in \mathfrak{C}^{C}$, and so we have $\alpha 1=1$. We shall show that

$$
\overline{\alpha e_{k}}=-\alpha e_{k}, \quad k=1,2, \cdots, 7 .
$$

Note that $\left(\alpha e_{k}\right)\left(\alpha e_{k}\right)=\alpha\left(e_{k} e_{k}\right)=\alpha(-1)=-1$. Let $x=\alpha e_{k}$ and $N(x)=x \bar{x} \in C$. Then $x x=-1$ and $N(x) N(x)=1$, which shows that $N(x)= \pm 1$. If $N(x)=-1$, then $\bar{x}=-x x \bar{x}=-x N(x)=x$, so $x \in C$. From $x x=-1$, we have $x= \pm i$. Then $\alpha\left(e_{k}\right)= \pm \alpha(i)$, and so $e_{k}= \pm i$, which is a contradiction. Hence $N(x)=1$, that is, $x \bar{x}=1$ and $\bar{x}=-x x \bar{x}=-x N(x)=-x$. Thus we have

$$
\overline{\alpha x}=\alpha \bar{x}, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C}^{C} .
$$

Now we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\alpha x, \alpha y) & =\frac{1}{2}((\alpha x)(\overline{\alpha y})+(\alpha y)(\overline{\alpha x}))=\frac{1}{2}((\alpha x)(\alpha \bar{y})+(\alpha y)(\alpha \bar{x})) \\
& =\alpha\left(\frac{1}{2}(\alpha(x \bar{y}+y \bar{x}))=\alpha((x, y))=(x, y) .\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

We define a positive definite Hermitian inner product $\langle x, y\rangle$ in $\mathfrak{C}^{C}$ by

$$
\langle x, y\rangle=(\tau x, y),
$$

For $\alpha \in \operatorname{Hom}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)$, we denote the complex conjugate transpose of $\alpha$ with respect to the inner product $\langle x, y\rangle$ by $\alpha^{*}:\left\langle\alpha^{*} x, y\right\rangle=\langle x, \alpha y\rangle$.

Lemma 1.12.2. (1) For $\alpha \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$, we have $\alpha^{*}=\tau \alpha^{-1} \tau \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$.
(2) For any $\alpha \in G_{2}$, its complexificated mapping $\alpha^{C}: \mathfrak{C}^{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}^{C}$ belongs to $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ : $\alpha^{C} \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$. Identifying $\alpha$ with $\alpha^{C}$, we regard $G_{2}$ as a subgroup of $G_{2}{ }^{C}: G_{2} \subset G_{2}{ }^{C}$. Now, for $\alpha \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$, we have, $\alpha \in G_{2}$ if and only if $\tau \alpha=\alpha \tau$, that is,

$$
G_{2}=\left\{\alpha \in G_{2}^{C} \mid \tau \alpha=\alpha \tau\right\} .
$$

Proof. (1) $\left\langle\alpha^{*} x, y\right\rangle=\langle x, \alpha y\rangle=(\tau x, \alpha y)=\left(\alpha^{-1} \tau x, y\right)=\left\langle\tau \alpha^{-1} \tau x, y\right\rangle$ for all $x, y \in \mathfrak{C}^{C}$. Hence $\alpha^{*}=\tau \alpha^{-1} \tau \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$.
(2) Let $\alpha \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$ satisfy $\tau \alpha=\alpha \tau$. Then since $\tau \alpha x=\alpha \tau x=\alpha x$, we have $\alpha x \in \mathfrak{C}$ for $x \in \mathfrak{C}$. Hence $\alpha$ induces an $\boldsymbol{R}$-transformation $\alpha^{\prime}$ of $\mathfrak{C}$ and $\alpha^{\prime} \in G_{2}$, further we have $\alpha=\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{C}$.

Theorem 1.12.3. The polar decomposition of the group $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is given by

$$
G_{2}{ }^{C} \simeq G_{2} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{14} .
$$

In particular, $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is a simply connected complex Lie group of type $G_{2}$.
Proof. Evidently $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is an algebraic subgroup of $\operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)=G L(8, C)$. If $\alpha \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$, then $\alpha^{*} \in G_{2}{ }^{C}$ (Lemma 1.12.2.(1)). Hence, from Chevalley's lemma (Chevalley [5]), we have

$$
G_{2}{ }^{C} \simeq\left(G_{2}{ }^{C} \cap U\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)\right) \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d}=G_{2} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d},
$$

where $U\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right) \mid\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\}$ and $d=\operatorname{dim} G_{2}{ }^{C}-\operatorname{dim} G_{2}=$ $2 \times 14-14=14$. Since $G_{2}$ is simply connected (Theorem 1.9.3), $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is also simply connected. The Lie algebra of the group $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is $\mathfrak{g}_{2}{ }^{C}$, so that $G_{2}{ }^{C}$ is a complex simple Lie group of type $G_{2}$.

### 1.13. Non-compact exceptional Lie group $G_{2(2)}$ of type $G_{2}$

In $\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}{ }^{\prime}$, we define a multiplication by

$$
\left(m+a e_{4}{ }^{\prime}\right)\left(n+b e_{4}{ }^{\prime}\right)=(m n+\bar{b} a)+(a \bar{n}+b m) e_{4}{ }^{\prime} .
$$

This algebra $\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}$ is called the split Cayley algebra, and is isomorphic to $\left(\mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)_{\tau \gamma}=\{x \in$ $\left.\mathfrak{C}^{C} \mid \tau \gamma x=x\right\}$ as algebras. Now, the group $G_{2(2)}$ is defined to be the automorphism group of the split Cayley algebras $\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}$ :

$$
G_{2(2)}=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right) \mid \alpha(x y)=(\alpha x)(\alpha y)\right\} .
$$

and which can also be defined by

$$
G_{2(2)}=\left(G_{2}{ }^{C}\right)^{\tau \gamma}=\left\{\alpha \in G_{2}{ }^{C} \mid \tau \gamma \alpha=\alpha \gamma \tau\right\} .
$$

Theorem 1.13.1. The polar decomposition of the Lie groups $G_{2(2)}$ is given by

$$
G_{2(2)} \simeq(S p(1) \times S p(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{8} .
$$

Proof. In the split Cayley algebra $\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}$, the inner product $(x, y)^{\prime}$ is defined as $(x, y)^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(x \bar{y}+y \bar{x})$. If we define an inner product $(x, y)$ of $\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}$ by $(\gamma x, y)^{\prime}$, then $(x, y)$ is a positive definite inner product. For $\alpha \in G_{2(2)}$, the transpose ${ }^{t} \alpha$ of $\alpha$ with respect to the inner product $(x, y)$ is ${ }^{t} \alpha=\gamma \alpha^{-1} \gamma \in G_{2(2)}$. Since $G_{2(2)}$ is an algebraic subgroup of $\operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right)=G L(8, \boldsymbol{R})$, from Chevalley's lemma, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
G_{2(2)} & \simeq\left(G_{2(2)} \cap O(8)\right) \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d} \\
& =\left(\left(G_{2}{ }^{C}\right)^{\tau \gamma}\right)^{\gamma} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d}=\left(\left(G_{2}{ }^{C}\right)^{\tau}\right)^{\gamma} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d}=\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d} \\
& =(\operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(1)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d}(\text { Theorem 1.10.1 }), \quad d=8 .
\end{aligned}
$$

where $O(8)=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right) \mid(\alpha x, \alpha y)=(x, y)\right\}$.
Theorem 1.13.2. The center $z\left(G_{2(2)}\right)$ of the group $G_{2(2)}$ is trivial:

$$
z\left(G_{2(2)}\right)=\{1\} .
$$

### 1.14. Principle of triality in $S O(8)$

For $a \in S^{n-1}=\left\{a \in \boldsymbol{R}^{n} \mid(a, a)=1\right\}$, we define an element $D_{a} \in O(n)=$ $O\left(\boldsymbol{R}^{n}\right)=\left\{A \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\boldsymbol{R}^{n}\right) \mid(A x, A y)=(x, y)\right\}$ by

$$
D_{a} x=x-2(x, a) a, \quad x \in \boldsymbol{R}^{n} .
$$

$D_{a}$ is called the reflection with respect to the hyperplane orthogonal to $a$. Its determinant is -1: $\operatorname{det}\left(D_{a}\right)=-1$.

Lemma 1.14.1. The group $O(n)$ is generated by reflections, that is, any $A \in O(n)$ can be expressed by the product of finite number of reflections:

$$
A=D_{a_{m}} \cdots D_{a_{2}} D_{a_{1}}, \quad a_{i} \in S^{n-1}
$$

In particular, $A \in S O(n)$ can be expressed by the product of even number of reflections.

$$
A=D_{a_{2 m}} \cdots D_{a_{2}} D_{a_{1}}, \quad a_{i} \in S^{n-1}
$$

Proof. (See Theorem 24 of Yokota [58).
From now on, the group $S O(8)$ is identified with the group

$$
S O(\mathfrak{C})=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{C}) \mid(\alpha x, \alpha y)=(x, y), \operatorname{det} \alpha=1\right\} .
$$

Theorem 1.14.2. (Principle of triality in $S O(8)$ ). For any $\alpha_{3} \in S O(8)$, there exist $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2} \in S O(8)$ such that

$$
\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=\alpha_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Moreover, $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}$ are determined uniquely for $\alpha_{3}$ up to the sign, that is, for $\alpha_{3}$, such $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}$ have to be $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}$ or $-\alpha_{1},-\alpha_{2}$.

Proof. Since $\alpha_{3} \in S O(8)$ is expressed by the product of even number of reflections (Lemma 1.14.1), it is sufficient to show the existence of $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}$ only for $\alpha_{3}=D_{b} D_{a}, a, b \in \mathfrak{C},|a|=|b|=1$. Now, since

$$
D_{a} x=x-2(x, a) a=x-(x \bar{a}+a \bar{x}) a=-a \bar{x} a, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C},
$$

we have $\alpha_{3} x=D_{b} D_{a} x=b(\bar{a} x \bar{a}) b$. Define mappings $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ respectively by $\alpha_{1} x=b(\bar{a} x), \alpha_{2} x=(x \bar{a}) b$. We then see that $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2} \in S O(8)$ and

$$
\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=(b(\bar{a} x))((y \bar{a}) b)=b(\bar{a}(x y) \bar{a}) b=\alpha_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Next, we shall show the uniqueness of $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}$ up to the sign. To prove this, it is sufficient to show in the case $\alpha_{3}=1$. Now, let

$$
\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=x y, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Let $\alpha_{1} 1=p$, then $|p|=1$ and $p\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=y$, so $\alpha_{2} y=\bar{p} y$. Similarly we have $\alpha_{1} x=x \bar{q}$, where $q=\alpha_{2} 1$. Hence $(x \bar{q})(\bar{p} y)=x y$. If we let $x=y=1$, then $\bar{q} \bar{p}=1$, so $\bar{q}=p$. Therefore we have

$$
(x p)(\bar{p} y)=x y, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Putting $p y$ instead of $y$, we have

$$
(x p) y=x(p y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

From this, we see that $p$ is a real number. Hence $p= \pm 1$ because $|p|=1$. Thus we have $\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=1$ or $\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=-1$.

Lemma 1.14.3. For $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3} \in O(8)$, the relation

$$
\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=\overline{\alpha_{3}(\overline{x y})}, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C}
$$

implies

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
\left(\alpha_{2} x\right)\left(\alpha_{3} y\right)=\overline{\alpha_{1}(\overline{x y})}, & x, y \in \mathfrak{C}, \\
\left(\alpha_{3} x\right)\left(\alpha_{1} y\right)=\overline{\alpha_{2}(\overline{x y})}, & x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
\end{array}
$$

Proof. If $x=0$ or $y=0$, then the statement is trivially valid, so we may assume $x, y \neq 0$. Now, multiply $\overline{\alpha_{1} x}$ from left and $\alpha_{3}(\overline{x y})$ from right on the relation
$\left.\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=\overline{\alpha_{3}(\overline{x y})}\right)$. Then we get $|x|^{2}\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)\left(\alpha_{3}(\overline{x y})\right)=\overline{\alpha_{1} x}|x y|^{2}$. Therefore

$$
\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)\left(\alpha_{3}(\overline{x y})\right)=\overline{\alpha_{1} x}|y|^{2} .
$$

Putting $\overline{y z}$ instead of $x$, we have $\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)\left(\alpha_{3}(\bar{y} y z)\right)=\overline{\alpha_{1}(\overline{y z})}|y|^{2}$, that is,

$$
\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)\left(\alpha_{3} z\right)=\overline{\alpha_{1}(\overline{y z})} .
$$

The other relation is similarly obtained.
Lemma 1.14.4. If $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3} \in O(8)$ satisfy

$$
\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=\alpha_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C},
$$

then $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3} \in S O(8)$.
Proof. Suppose $\alpha_{1} \notin S O(8)$. Since the mapping $\epsilon: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ defined by $\epsilon x=\bar{x}$ belongs to $O(8)$, and since $\operatorname{det} \epsilon=-1$, we have $\beta_{1}=\epsilon \alpha_{1}^{-1} \in S O(8)$. Using the principle of triality (Theorem 1.14.2) on the element $\beta_{1}$ (cf. Lemma 1.14.3), there exist $\beta_{2}, \beta_{3} \in S O(8)$ such that

$$
\left(\beta_{1}\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\right)\left(\beta_{2}\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)\right)=\beta_{3}\left(\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)\right)=\beta_{3}\left(\alpha_{3}(x y)\right), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Setting $\beta_{2} \alpha_{2}=\gamma_{2}$ and $\beta_{3} \alpha_{3}=\gamma_{3}$, the above relation becomes

$$
\bar{x}\left(\gamma_{2} y\right)=\gamma_{3}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Put $x=1$, then $\gamma_{2} y=\gamma_{3} y$, so $\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{3}$. Hence we have

$$
\bar{x}\left(\gamma_{2} y\right)=\gamma_{2}(x y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Put $\gamma_{2} 1=p$, then $|p|=1$. Lett $y=1$ in (i), then $\bar{x} p=\gamma_{2} x$. Hence (i) becomes

$$
\bar{x}(\bar{y} p)=(\overline{x y}) p, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Again let $y=p$, then $\bar{x}=(\overline{x p}) p$, and so $\bar{x} \bar{p}=\overline{x p}$. Then

$$
p x=x p \quad \text { for all } x \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Therefore $p \in \boldsymbol{R}$, hence $p= \pm 1$ since $|p|=1$. Thus (ii) becomes $\bar{x} \bar{y}=\overline{x y}$, and so

$$
x y=y x \quad \text { for all } x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

But this is a contradiction. Therefore $\alpha_{1} \in S O(8)$. As for $\alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}$, use Lemma 1.14.3 and the argument above, to show $\alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3} \in S O(8)$.

As a corollary of the principle of triality (Theorem 1.14.2), we have the following proposition.

Proposition 1.14.5. For $a \in \mathfrak{C}$ such that $|a|=1$, the mapping $\alpha_{a}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ defined by

$$
\alpha_{a} x=a x a^{-1}, \quad x \in \mathfrak{C}
$$

belongs to the group $G_{2}$ if and only if $a^{3}= \pm 1$.
Proof. If we apply Lemma 1.14.3 to the Moufang formula $(\bar{a} x)(y \bar{a})=\bar{a}(x y) \bar{a}$, then

$$
(x \bar{a})(a y a)=(x y) a, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$
