& (-\mu,-\mu)=\frac{1}{9}, \quad\left(-\mu, \alpha_{1}\right)=-\frac{1}{18}, \quad\left(-\mu, \alpha_{i}\right)=0, \quad i=2,3,4
\end{aligned}
$$

using them, we can draw the Dynkin diagram and the extended Dynkin diagram of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$.

According Borel-Siebenthal theory, the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{f}_{4}$ has three subalgebras as maximal subalgebras with the maximal rank 4.
(1) The first one is a subalgebra of type $B_{4}$ obtained as the fixed points of an involution $\sigma$ of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}$.
(2) The second one is a subalgebra of type $C_{1} \oplus C_{3}$ obtained as the fixed points of an involution $\gamma$ of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}$.
(3) The third one is a subalgebra of type $A_{2} \oplus A_{2}$ obtained as the fixed points of an automorphism $w$ of order 3 of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}$.

These subalgebras will be realized as subgroups of the group $F_{4}$ in Theorems 2.9.1, 2.11.2 and 2.12.2, respectively.
2.7. Subgroup $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ of $F_{4}$

Theorem 2.7.1. $\left\{\alpha \in F_{4} \mid \alpha E_{i}=E_{i}, i=1,2,3\right\} \cong \operatorname{Spin}(8)$.
(From now on, we identify these groups).
Proof. We recall the group $\operatorname{Spin}(8)$ of Theorem 1.16.2 and define a mapping $\varphi: \operatorname{Spin}(8) \rightarrow D_{4}=\left\{\alpha \in F_{4} \mid \alpha E_{i}=E_{i}, i=1,2,3\right\}$ by

$$
\varphi\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right)\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & \alpha_{3} x_{3} & \overline{\alpha_{2} x_{2}} \\
\overline{\alpha_{3} x_{3}} & \xi_{2} & \alpha_{1} x_{1} \\
\alpha_{2} x_{2} & \overline{\alpha_{1} x_{1}} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

We first prove that $\alpha=\varphi\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right) \in D_{4}$. Indeed, the fact that $\operatorname{det}(\alpha X)=\operatorname{det} X$ can be seen by observing that

$$
\begin{aligned}
& R\left(\left(\alpha_{1} x_{1}\right)\left(\alpha_{2} x_{2}\right)\left(\alpha_{3} x_{3}\right)\right)=R\left(\left(\overline{\alpha_{3}\left(\overline{x_{1} x_{2}}\right)}\right) \alpha_{3} x_{3}\right) \\
& \quad=\left(\alpha_{3}\left(\overline{x_{1} x_{2}}\right), \alpha_{3} x_{3}\right)=\left(\overline{x_{1} x_{2}}, x_{3}\right)=R\left(x_{1} x_{2} x_{3}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

which together with $\alpha E=E$, shows that $\alpha \in F_{4}$ and $\alpha E_{i}=E_{i}, i=1,2,3$. Therefore $\alpha \in D_{4}$. Certainly $\varphi$ is a homomorphism. We shall show that $\varphi$ is onto. Let $\alpha \in D_{4}$. We put

$$
\mathfrak{J}_{i}=\left\{F_{i}(x) \mid x \in \mathfrak{C}\right\}=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid 2 E_{i+1} \circ X=2 E_{i+2} \circ X=X\right\}, \quad i=1,2,3 .
$$

Since $\alpha X \in \mathfrak{J}_{i}, X \in \mathfrak{J}_{i}, \alpha$ induces $\boldsymbol{R}$-isomorphisms $\alpha: \mathfrak{J}_{i} \rightarrow \mathfrak{J}_{i}$ and $\alpha_{i}: \mathfrak{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{C}$ satisfying

$$
\alpha F_{i}(x)=F_{i}\left(\alpha_{i} x\right), \quad x \in \mathfrak{C},
$$

for $i=1,2,3$. Applying $\alpha$ on $F_{i}(x) \circ F_{i}(y)=(x, y)\left(E_{i+1}+E_{i+2}\right)$, since the left side becomes $F_{i}\left(\alpha_{i} x\right) \circ F_{i}\left(\alpha_{i} y\right)=\left(\alpha_{i} x, \alpha_{i} y\right)\left(E_{i+1}+E_{i+2}\right)$, we have

$$
\left(\alpha_{i} x, \alpha_{i} y\right)=(x, y), \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

Hence $\alpha_{i} \in O(8), i=1,2,3$. Moreover, by applying $\alpha$ on $F_{1}(x) \circ F_{2}(y)=\frac{1}{2} F_{3}(\overline{x y})$, we see that

$$
\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)\left(\alpha_{2} y\right)=\overline{\alpha_{3}(\overline{x y})}, \quad x, y \in \mathfrak{C} .
$$

From Lemma 1.14.4, we have $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3} \in S O(8)$, so that $\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right) \in \operatorname{Spin}(8)$ and $\varphi\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right)=\alpha$. Therefore $\varphi$ is onto. Evidently $\operatorname{Ker} \varphi=\{(1,1,1)\}$. Consequently $\varphi$ is an isomorphism.

We shall study the following subgroup $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ of $F_{4}$ :

$$
\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}=\left\{\alpha \in F_{4} \mid \alpha E_{1}=E_{1}\right\} .
$$

We define $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector subspaces $\mathfrak{J}_{01}, \mathfrak{J}_{23}$ of $\mathfrak{J}$ respectively by

$$
\mathfrak{J}_{01}=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid E_{1} \circ X=0, \operatorname{tr}(X)=0\right\}=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
0 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \xi & x \\
0 & \bar{x} & -\xi
\end{array}\right) \right\rvert\, \xi \in \boldsymbol{R}, x \in \mathfrak{C}\right\},
$$

$$
\mathfrak{J}_{23}=\left\{Y \in \mathfrak{J} \mid 2 E_{1} \circ Y=Y\right\}=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
0 & y_{3} & \bar{y}_{2} \\
\bar{y}_{3} & 0 & 0 \\
y_{2} & 0 & 0
\end{array}\right) \right\rvert\, y_{2}, y_{3} \in \mathfrak{C}\right\} .
$$

Lemma 2.7.2. Suppose that we are given an element $A \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ such that $(A, A)=$ 2. Choose any element $X_{0} \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ such that $\left(X_{0}, X_{0}\right)=2,\left(A, X_{0}\right)=0$. Choose any element $Y_{0} \in \mathfrak{J}_{23}$ such that $\left(Y_{0}, Y_{0}\right)=2,2 A \circ Y_{0}=-Y_{0}$. Let

$$
Z_{0}=2 X_{0} \circ Y_{0} .
$$

Choose any element $X_{1} \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ such that $\left(X_{1}, X_{1}\right)=2,\left(A, X_{1}\right)=\left(X_{0}, X_{1}\right)=0$. Choose any element $X_{2} \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ such that $\left(X_{2}, X_{2}\right)=2,\left(A, X_{2}\right)=\left(X_{0}, X_{2}\right)=$ $\left(X_{1}, X_{2}\right)=0$. Let

$$
Y_{1}=-2 Z_{0} \circ X_{1}, \quad Z_{2}=-2 X_{2} \circ Y_{0}, \quad X_{3}=-2 Y_{1} \circ Z_{2} .
$$

Finally, choose any element $X_{4} \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ such that $\left(X_{4}, X_{4}\right)=2,\left(A, X_{4}\right)=\left(X_{0}, X_{4}\right)=$ $\left(X_{1}, X_{4}\right)=\left(X_{2}, X_{4}\right)=\left(X_{3}, X_{4}\right)=0$. Let

$$
\begin{array}{lll}
Z_{4}=-2 X_{4} \circ Y_{0}, & Y_{2}=-2 Z_{0} \circ X_{2}, & Y_{3}=-2 Z_{0} \circ X_{3}, \\
X_{5}=-2 Y_{1} \circ Z_{4}, & X_{6}=2 Y_{2} \circ Z_{4}, & X_{7}=-2 Y_{3} \circ Z_{4}
\end{array}
$$

and moreover let

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
Y_{i}=-2 Z_{0} \circ X_{i}, & i=4,5,6,7, \\
Z_{i}=-2 X_{i} \circ Y_{0}, & i=1,3,5,6,7 .
\end{array}
$$

Then, an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear mapping $\alpha: \mathfrak{J} \rightarrow \mathfrak{J}$ satisfying

$$
\begin{array}{lll}
\alpha E=E, & \alpha E_{1}=E_{1}, & \alpha\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=A, \\
\alpha F_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)=X_{i}, & \alpha F_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)=Y_{i}, & \alpha F_{3}\left(e_{i}\right)=Z_{i}, \quad i=0,1, \cdots, 7
\end{array}
$$

belongs to $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$.
Proof. We have to show that the setting of the lemma is appropriate and prove that $\alpha$ satisfies

$$
\alpha(X \circ Y)=\alpha X \circ \alpha Y, \quad X, Y \in \mathfrak{J} .
$$

For this purpose, it is sufficient to show that this holds for generators $X, Y=E_{i}$, $F_{j}\left(e_{k}\right)$ of $\mathfrak{J}$. We have to check $27^{2}=729$ times if honestly doing so we reduce the number of times after preparing some lemma. However we omit its proof. In details, see Yokota [40].

Proposition 2.7.3. $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} / \operatorname{Spin}(8) \simeq S^{8}$.
In particular, the group $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ is connected.
Proof. $S^{8}=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J}_{01} \mid(X, X)=2\right\}$ is an 8 dimentional sphere. For $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ and $X \in S^{8}$, we have $\alpha X \in S^{8}$. Hence the group $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ acts on $S^{8}$. This action is transitive. Indeed, for a given $A \in S^{8}$, by constructing $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ of Lemma 2.7.2,
we have $\alpha\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=A$, and which shows the transitivity. We determine the isotropy subgroup of $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ at $E_{2}-E_{3} \in S^{8}$. Let $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ satisfy $\alpha\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=E_{2}-E_{3}$. Since $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ satisfies $\alpha E_{1}=E_{1}$ and $\alpha E=E$, it also satisfies $\alpha\left(E_{2}+E_{3}\right)=E_{2}+$ $E_{3}$. Therefore we have $\alpha E_{2}=E_{2}$ and $\alpha E_{3}=E_{3}$, so that $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(8)$. Conversely, $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(8)$ satisfies $\alpha\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=E_{2}-E_{3}$. Thus we have the homeomorphism $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} / \operatorname{Spin}(8) \simeq S^{8}$.

Remark. If we know the dimension of the group $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$, without using Lemma 2.7.2, Proposition 2.7.3 can be simply proved as follows. The group $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ acts on $S^{8}$. The isotropy subgroup of $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ at $E_{2}-E_{3}$ is $\operatorname{Spin}(8)$ and $\operatorname{dim}\left(\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} / \operatorname{Spin}(8)\right)=$ $\operatorname{dim}\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}-\operatorname{dim} \operatorname{Spin}(8)=36-28=\operatorname{dim} S^{8}$. Therefore, we have $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} / \operatorname{Spin}(8) \simeq$ $S^{8}$.

Theorem 2.7.4. $\quad\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \cong \operatorname{Spin}(9)$.
(From now on, we identify these groups).
Proof. Let $O(9)=O\left(\mathfrak{J}_{01}\right)=\left\{\alpha^{\prime} \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{J}_{01}\right) \mid\left(\alpha^{\prime} X, \alpha^{\prime} Y\right)=(X, Y)\right\}$. Consider the restriction $\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha \mid \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ of $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ to $\mathfrak{J}_{01}$, then $\alpha^{\prime} \in O(9)$. Hence we can define a homomorphism $p:\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \rightarrow O(9)$ by $p(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}$. Since $p$ is continuous and $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ is connected (Proposition 2.7.3), the mapping $p$ induces a homomorphism

$$
p:\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \rightarrow S O(9) .
$$

We shall show $p$ is onto. Let $S O(8)=\left\{\alpha^{\prime} \in S O(9) \mid \alpha^{\prime}\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=E_{2}-E_{3}\right\}$. The restriction $p^{\prime}$ of $p:\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \rightarrow S O(9)$ to $\operatorname{Spin}(8)=\left\{\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \mid \alpha\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=E_{2}-E_{3}\right\}$ coincides with the homomorphism $p: \operatorname{Spin}(9) \rightarrow S O(8)$ in Theorem 1.16.2. In particular, $p^{\prime}: \operatorname{Spin}(8) \rightarrow S O(8)$ is onto. Hence, from the following commutative diagram

$$
\begin{array}{clcccccc}
1 & \longrightarrow & \operatorname{Spin}(8) & \longrightarrow & \left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} & \longrightarrow & S^{8} & \longrightarrow \\
& & \downarrow p^{\prime} & & \downarrow p & & \downarrow= & \\
1 & \longrightarrow & S O(8) & \longrightarrow & S O(9) & \longrightarrow & S^{8} & \longrightarrow
\end{array} .
$$

we see that $p:\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \rightarrow S O(9)$ is onto by the five lemma. $\operatorname{Ker} p=\{1, \sigma\}$, where $\sigma=$ $\varphi(1,-1,-1)$ (which corresponds to $\sigma$ defined in the following Section 2.9). Indeed, let $\alpha \in \operatorname{Ker} p$, then $\alpha$ satisfies $\alpha X=X$ for all $X \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$. From $\alpha\left(E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=E_{2}-E_{3}$ follows that $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(8)$. Let $\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right) \in \operatorname{Spin}(8)$. Since $\alpha F_{1}(x)=F_{1}(x)$, that is, $F_{1}\left(\alpha_{1} x\right)=F_{1}(x)$, so $\alpha_{1} x=x$ for all $x \in \mathfrak{C}$, hence we have $\alpha_{1}=1$. From the principle of triality, we have $\alpha=(1,1,1)=1$ or $\alpha=(1,-1,-1)=\sigma$. Hence we have $\operatorname{Ker} p=\{1, \sigma\}$, and so we have the isomorphism

$$
\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} /\{1, \sigma\} \cong S O(9) .
$$

Therefore $\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$ is isomorphic to the group $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ as the universal covering group of $S O(9)$.

Theorem 2.7.5. $\quad \operatorname{Spin}(9) / \operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7) \simeq S^{15}$.
where $\operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7)=\{\widetilde{\alpha} \in \operatorname{SO}(8) \mid(\widetilde{\alpha} x)(\alpha y)=\widetilde{\alpha}(x y), x, y \in \mathfrak{C}$ for some $\alpha \in S O(7)\}$.
Proof. Let $S^{15}=\left\{Y \in \mathfrak{J}_{23} \mid(Y, Y)=2\right\}$. For $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(9)$ and $Y \in S^{15}$, we have $\alpha Y \in S^{15}$. Hence $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ acts on $S^{15}$. We shall show that this action is transitive. Let $Y_{0} \in S^{15}$. Choose any element $A \in \mathfrak{J}_{01}$ such that $(A, A)=2,2 A \circ Y_{0}=-Y_{0}$. Using these $A$ and $Y_{0}$, construct $X_{i}, Y_{i}, Z_{i}$ and $\alpha$ of Lemma 2.7.2, then $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(9)$ and satisfies $\alpha F_{2}(1)=Y_{0}$, which shows the transitivity. We determine the isotropy subgroup $\operatorname{Spin}(9)_{F_{2}(1)}$ of $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ at $F_{2}(1) \in S^{15}$. Let $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(9)$ satisfies $\alpha F_{2}(1)=$ $F_{2}(1)$. Applying $\alpha$ on $F_{2}(1) \circ F_{2}(1)=E_{1}+E_{3}$ we have $F_{2}(1) \circ F_{2}(1)=E_{1}+\alpha E_{3}$, so we get $\alpha E_{3}=E_{3}$. Since $\alpha$ always satisfies $\alpha\left(E_{2}+E_{3}\right)=E_{2}+E_{3}$, we have $\alpha E_{2}=E_{2}$, and so $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(8)$. Denote $\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right)$, then $F_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}(1)\right)=F_{2}(1)$ implies $\alpha_{2} 1=1$, so that $\alpha_{2} \in S O(7)$. Hence $\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2}, \alpha_{3}\right) \in \operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7)$. Conversely $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(7)$ satisfies $\alpha F_{2}(1)=F_{2}(1)$. Therefore $\operatorname{Spin}(9)_{F_{2}(1)}=\operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7)$ is proved. Thus we have the homeomorphism $\operatorname{Spin}(9) / \operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7) \simeq S^{15}$.
$\left(\operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7)\right.$ and $\operatorname{Spin}(7)$ are conjugate in the group $O(7)$. Indeed, a mapping $f:$ $\operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7) \rightarrow \operatorname{Spin}(7)$,

$$
f\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)=\varepsilon \alpha^{\prime} \varepsilon^{-1}, \quad(\epsilon x=\bar{x}, x \in \mathfrak{C})
$$

gives the conjugation. In particular, $\left.\operatorname{Spin}^{\prime}(7) \cong \operatorname{Spin}(7)\right)$

### 2.8. Connectedness of $F_{4}$

We denote by $\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ the connected component of $F_{4}$ containing the identity 1 .
Lemma 2.8.1. For $a \in \mathfrak{C}$, we define a mapping $\beta_{1}(a): \mathfrak{J} \rightarrow \mathfrak{J}$ by $\beta_{1}(a) X(\xi, x)=$ $Y(\eta, y)$, where

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left\{\begin{array}{l}
\eta_{1}=\xi_{1} \\
\eta_{2}=\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}+\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2} \cos 2|a|+\frac{\left(a, x_{1}\right)}{|a|} \sin 2|a| \\
\eta_{3}=\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}-\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2} \cos 2|a|-\frac{\left(a, x_{1}\right)}{|a|} \sin 2|a|,
\end{array}\right. \\
& \left\{\begin{array}{l}
y_{1}=x_{1}-\frac{\left(\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}\right) a}{2|a|} \sin 2|a|-\frac{2\left(a, x_{1}\right) a}{|a|^{2}} \sin ^{2}|a| \\
y_{2}=x_{2} \cos |a|-\frac{\overline{x_{3} a}}{|a|} \sin |a| \\
y_{3}=x_{3} \cos |a|+\frac{\overline{a x}}{|a|} \sin |a|
\end{array}\right.
\end{aligned}
$$

(if $a=0$, then $\frac{\sin |a|}{|a|}$ means 1), then $\beta_{1}(a) \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$.
Proof. For $A_{1}(a)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & a \\ 0 & -\bar{a} & 0\end{array}\right)$, we have $\widetilde{A}_{1}(a) \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}$ (Proposition 2.3.6) and
$\beta_{1}(a)=\exp \widetilde{A}_{1}(a)$. Hence $\beta_{1}(a) \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$.
Proposition 2.8.2. Any element $X \in \mathfrak{J}$ can be transformed to a diagonal form by some element $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ :

$$
\alpha X=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \xi_{2} & 0 \\
0 & 0 & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right), \quad \xi_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}
$$

Moreover, $\xi_{1}, \xi_{2}, \xi_{3}$ are uniquely determined (up to their permutation) independent of the choice of $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$.

Proof. For a given $X \in \mathfrak{J}$, consider a space $\mathfrak{X}=\left\{\alpha X \mid \alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}\right\}$. Since $\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ is compact (Theorem 2.2.5), $\mathfrak{X}$ is also compact. Let $\xi_{1}{ }^{2}+\xi_{2}{ }^{2}+\xi_{3}{ }^{2}$ be the maximal value of all $\eta_{1}{ }^{2}+\eta_{2}{ }^{2}+\eta_{3}{ }^{2}$ for $Y=Y(\eta, y) \in \mathfrak{X}$ and let $X_{0}=X(\xi, x)$ be an element of $\mathfrak{X}$ which attains its maximal value. Then $X_{0}$ is of diagonal form. Certainly, suppose $X_{0}$ is not of diagonal form, for example, the $2 \times 3$ entry $x_{1}$ of $X_{0}$ is non-zero: $x_{1} \neq 0$. Let $a(t)=\frac{x_{1}}{\left|x_{1}\right|} t, t>0$, and construct $\beta_{1}(a(t)) \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ of Lemma 2.8.1. Since $|a(t)|=t$ and $\frac{\left(a(t), x_{1}\right)}{|a(t)|}=\left|x_{1}\right|$ for $Y(\eta(t), y(t))=\beta_{1}(a(t)) X_{0} \in \mathfrak{X}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \eta_{1}(t)^{2}+\eta_{2}(t)^{2}+\eta_{3}(t)^{2} \\
& =\xi_{1}{ }^{2}+\left(\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}+\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2} \cos 2 t+\left|x_{1}\right| \sin 2 t\right)^{2} \\
& \quad+\left(\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}-\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2} \cos 2 t-\left|x_{1}\right| \sin 2 t\right)^{2} \\
& =\xi_{1}{ }^{2}+2\left(\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}+2\left(\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2} \cos 2 t+\left|x_{1}\right| \sin 2 t\right)^{2} \\
& =\xi_{1}{ }^{2}+2\left(\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}+2\left(\left(\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left|x_{1}\right|^{2}\right) \sin ^{2}\left(2 t+t_{0}\right)\left(\text { for some } t_{0} \in \boldsymbol{R}\right) \\
& \leq{\xi_{1}}^{2}+2\left(\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}+2\left(\left(\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left|x_{1}\right|^{2}\right) \\
& ={\xi_{1}}^{2}+{\xi_{2}}^{2}+{\xi_{3}}^{2}+2\left|x_{1}\right|^{2}
\end{aligned}
$$

which is the maximal value and attains at some $t>0$. This contradicts the maximum of $\xi_{1}{ }^{2}+\xi_{2}{ }^{2}+\xi_{3}{ }^{2}$. Hence $x_{1}=0 . x_{2}=x_{3}=0$ can be similarly proved by constructing $\beta_{2}(a), \beta_{3}(a) \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ analogous to $\beta_{1}(a)$ of Lemma 2.8.1. Hence $X_{0}$ is of diagonal form. We now give the proof of the latter half of the proposition. If $X \in \mathfrak{J}$ is transformed to a diagonal form $\alpha X=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}\xi_{1} & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \xi_{2} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \xi_{3}\end{array}\right)$ by $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$, then

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \operatorname{tr}(X)=\operatorname{tr}(\alpha X)(\text { Lemma 2.2.1.(2) })=\sum_{i=1}^{3} \xi_{i}, \\
& (X, X)=(\alpha X, \alpha X)(\text { Lemma 2.2.4 })=\sum_{i=1}^{3} \xi_{i}{ }^{2}, \\
& \operatorname{tr}(X, X, X)=\operatorname{tr}(\alpha X, \alpha X, \alpha X)(\text { Lemma 2.2.4 })=\sum_{i=1}^{3} \xi_{i}{ }^{3} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence, $\xi_{1}, \xi_{2}, \xi_{3}$ are uniquely determined (up to order) as the solutions of the following simultaneous equation:

$$
\left\{\begin{array}{l}
\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\operatorname{tr}(X) \\
\xi_{1}{ }^{2}+\xi_{2}{ }^{2}+\xi_{3}{ }^{2}=(X, X) \\
\xi_{1}{ }^{3}+\xi_{2}{ }^{3}+\xi_{3}{ }^{3}=\operatorname{tr}(X, X, X)
\end{array}\right.
$$

The space $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}$, called the Cayley projective plane, is defined as

$$
\mathfrak{C} P_{2}=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid X^{2}=X, \operatorname{tr}(X)=1\right\} .
$$

Theorem 2.8.3.

$$
F_{4} / \operatorname{Spin}(9) \simeq \mathfrak{C} P_{2} .
$$

In particular, the group $F_{4}$ is connected.
Proof. For $\alpha \in F_{4}$ and $X \in \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$, we have $\alpha X \in \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$. Hence $F_{4}$ acts on $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}$. We shall prove that the group $\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ acts transitively on $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}$. To prove this, it is sufficient to show that any element $X \in \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ can be transformed to $E_{1} \in \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ by some $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$. Now, $X \in \mathfrak{C} P_{2} \subset \mathfrak{J}$ can be transformed to a diagonal form by $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ :

$$
\alpha X=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \xi_{2} & 0 \\
0 & 0 & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right), \quad \xi_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}
$$

(Proposition 2.8.2). From the condition $X \circ X=X$, we have $\alpha X \circ \alpha X=\alpha X$, that is,

$$
\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1}{ }^{2} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \xi_{2}{ }^{2} & 0 \\
0 & 0 & \xi_{3}{ }^{2}
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \xi_{2} & 0 \\
0 & 0 & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

Hence $\xi_{i}{ }^{2}=\xi_{i}$, so that $\xi_{i}=1$ or $\xi_{i}=0, i=1,2,3$. Next, from $\operatorname{tr}(\alpha X)=\operatorname{tr}(X)=1$, we have $\xi_{i}=1, \xi_{i+1}=\xi_{i+2}=0$ for some $i$, that is, $\alpha X=E_{i}$. Moreover, $E_{2}$, $E_{3}$ are transformed to $E_{1}$ respectively by $\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$. Certainly, if we define a mapping $\beta: \mathfrak{J} \rightarrow \mathfrak{J}, \beta X=T X T^{-1}$, where $T=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -1\end{array}\right) \in S O(3)$, then $\beta \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{0}$ and $\beta E_{2}=E_{1}$. Hence $\beta \alpha X=E_{1}$. In the case $\alpha X=E_{3}$, the situation is similar to the above. Therefore the transitivity is proved. Since we have $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}=\left(F_{4}\right)_{0} E_{1}, \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ is connected. Now, the group $F_{4}$ acts transitively on $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ and the isotropy subgroup of $F_{4}$ at $E_{1} \in \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ is $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ (Theorem 2.7.4). Thus we have the homeomorphism $F_{4} / \operatorname{Spin}(9) \simeq \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$. Finally, the connectedness of $F_{4}$ follows from the connectedness of $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ and $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$.

### 2.9. Involution $\sigma$ and subgroup $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ of $F_{4}$

Definition. We define an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear transformation $\sigma$ of $\mathfrak{J}$ by

$$
\sigma\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & -x_{3} & -\bar{x}_{2} \\
-\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
-x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

Then $\sigma \in F_{4}$ and $\sigma^{2}=1$. Observe that this $\sigma$ is the same as $\sigma=(1,-1,-1) \in$ $\operatorname{Spin}(8) \subset \operatorname{Spin}(9) \subset F_{4}$ of Theorem 2.7.4.

We shall study the following subgroup $\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}$ of $F_{4}$ :

$$
\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}=\left\{\alpha \in F_{4} \mid \sigma \alpha=\alpha \sigma\right\} .
$$

For this end, we consider $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector subspaces $\mathfrak{J}_{\sigma}$ and $\mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma}$ of $\mathfrak{J}$, which are eigenspaces of $\sigma$, respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak{J}_{\sigma} & =\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid \sigma X=X\}=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
0 & \overline{x_{1}} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) \right\rvert\, \xi_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}, x_{1} \in \mathfrak{C}\right\} \\
& \left.=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid E_{1} \circ X=0\right\} \oplus \mathfrak{E}_{1} \text { (where } \mathfrak{E}_{1}=\left\{\xi E_{1} \mid \xi \in \boldsymbol{R}\right\}\right) \\
& =\mathfrak{J}(2, \mathfrak{C}) \oplus \mathfrak{E}_{1}, \\
\mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma} & =\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid \sigma X=-X\}=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
0 & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & 0 & 0 \\
x_{2} & 0 & 0
\end{array}\right) \right\rvert\, x_{i} \in \mathfrak{C}\right\} \\
& =\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid 2 E_{1} \circ X=X\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Then, $\mathfrak{J}=\mathfrak{J}_{\sigma} \oplus \mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma}$ and $\mathfrak{J}_{\sigma}, \mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma}$ are invariant under the action of $\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}$.
Theorem 2.9.1. $\quad\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}=\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}=\operatorname{Spin}(9)$.
Proof. We shall show that for $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}$ we have $\alpha E_{1}=E_{1}$. Let $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}$. We first show that

$$
\alpha E_{2}, \alpha E_{3} \in \mathfrak{J}(2, \mathfrak{C}) .
$$

Certainly we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
\alpha E_{2} & =\alpha\left(-F_{2}(1) \times F_{2}(1)\right)=-\alpha F_{2}(1) \times \alpha F_{2}(1) \\
& =-\left(F_{2}\left(x_{2}\right)+F_{3}\left(x_{3}\right)\right) \times\left(F_{2}\left(x_{2}\right)+F_{3}\left(x_{3}\right)\right) \quad\left(\text { for some } x_{2}, x_{3} \in \mathfrak{C}\right) \\
& =\left(x_{2}, x_{2}\right) E_{2}+\left(x_{3}, x_{3}\right) E_{3}-F_{1}\left(\overline{x_{2} x_{3}}\right) \in \mathfrak{J}(2, \mathfrak{C})
\end{aligned}
$$

Similarly $\alpha E_{3} \in \mathfrak{J}(2, \mathfrak{C})$. So $\alpha E_{1}=\alpha\left(E-E_{2}-E_{3}\right)=E-\alpha E_{2}-\alpha E_{3}$ is of the form

$$
\alpha E_{1}=E_{1}+\xi_{2} E_{2}+\xi_{3} E_{3}+F_{1}\left(x_{1}\right) .
$$

Then we have

$$
1=\left(E_{1}, E_{1}\right)=\left(\alpha E_{1}, \alpha E_{1}\right)=1+\xi_{2}{ }^{2}+\xi_{3}{ }^{2}+2\left|x_{1}\right|^{2},
$$

so that $\xi_{2}=\xi_{3}=x_{1}=0$, therefore $\alpha E_{1}=E_{1}$, that is, $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$. Conversely let $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}}$, that is, $\alpha \in F_{4}$ satisfies $\alpha E_{1}=E_{1}$. Noting that $\mathfrak{J}=\mathfrak{J}_{\sigma} \oplus \mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma}$ and $\mathfrak{J}_{\sigma}=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid E_{1} \circ X=0\right\} \oplus \mathfrak{E}_{1}, \mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma}=\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid 2 E_{1} \circ X=X\right\}$ are invariant under $\alpha$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
\alpha \sigma X & =\alpha \sigma\left(X_{1}+X_{2}\right) \quad X_{1} \in \mathfrak{J}_{\sigma}, \quad X_{2} \in \mathfrak{J}_{-\sigma} \\
& =\alpha\left(X_{1}-X_{2}\right)=\alpha X_{1}-\alpha X_{2}=\sigma\left(\alpha X_{1}\right)+\sigma\left(\alpha X_{2}\right) \\
& =\sigma \alpha\left(X_{1}+X_{2}\right)=\sigma \alpha X, \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus $\alpha \sigma=\sigma \alpha$, and so $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}$. Therefore we have shown that $\left(F_{4}\right)^{\sigma}=\left(F_{4}\right)_{E_{1}} \cong$ $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ (Theorem 2.7.4).
2.10. Center $z\left(F_{4}\right)$ of $F_{4}$

Theorem 2.10.1. The center $z\left(F_{4}\right)$ of the group $F_{4}$ is trivial:

$$
z\left(F_{4}\right)=\{1\} .
$$

Proof. Let $\alpha \in z\left(F_{4}\right)$. From the commutativity with $\sigma: \sigma \alpha=\alpha \sigma$, we have $\alpha \in \operatorname{Spin}(9)$ (Theorem 2.9.1) and so $\alpha \in z(\operatorname{Spin}(9))$. Since the center $z(\operatorname{Spin}(9))$ of $\operatorname{Spin}(9)$ is the group of order 2, we have

$$
\alpha=1 \quad \text { or } \quad \alpha=\sigma .
$$

However, $\sigma \notin z\left(F_{4}\right)$ (Theorem 2.9.1), which implies that $\alpha=1$.
According to a general theory of compact Lie groups, it is known that the center of the simply connected simple Lie group of type $F_{4}$ is trivial. Hence $F_{4}$ has to be simply connected. Thus we have the following Theorem.

Theorem 2.10.2. $F_{4}=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{J}) \mid \alpha(X \circ Y)=\alpha X \circ \alpha Y\right\}$ is a simply connected compact Lie group of type $F_{4}$.

Remark. If we know that the space $\mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ is simply connected, the simply connectedness of the group $F_{4}$ follows from $F_{4} / \operatorname{Spin}(9) \simeq \mathfrak{C} P_{2}$ of Theorem 2.8.3.
2.11. Involution $\gamma$ and subgroup $(S p(1) \times S p(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ of $F_{4}$

We define an involutive $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear transformation $\gamma$ of $\mathfrak{J}$ by

$$
\gamma\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & \gamma x_{3} & \overline{\gamma x_{2}} \\
\overline{\gamma x_{3}} & \xi_{2} & \gamma x_{1} \\
\gamma x_{2} & \overline{\gamma x_{1}} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

This $\gamma$ is the same as $\gamma \in G_{2} \subset F_{4}$.

We shall study the following subgroup $\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$ of $F_{4}$ :

$$
\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}=\left\{\alpha \in F_{4} \mid \gamma \alpha=\alpha \gamma\right\} .
$$

Any element $X \in \mathfrak{J}$ is expressed by

$$
X=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & m_{3} & \bar{m}_{2} \\
\bar{m}_{3} & \xi_{2} & m_{1} \\
m_{2} & \bar{m}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
0 & a_{3} e_{4} & -a_{2} e_{4} \\
-a_{3} e_{4} & 0 & a_{1} e_{4} \\
a_{2} e_{4} & -a_{1} e_{4} & 0
\end{array}\right),
$$

where $x_{i}=m_{i}+a_{i} e_{4} \in \boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}=\mathfrak{C}$. We associate such element $X \in \mathfrak{J}$ with the element of $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{H}) \oplus \boldsymbol{H}^{3}$,

$$
\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & m_{3} & \bar{m}_{2} \\
\bar{m}_{3} & \xi_{2} & m_{1} \\
m_{2} & \bar{m}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)+\left(a_{1}, a_{2}, a_{3}\right) .
$$

In $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{H}) \oplus \boldsymbol{H}^{3}$, we define a multiplication ×, an inner product ( , ) and an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear transformation $\gamma$ respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
(M+\boldsymbol{a}) \times(N+\boldsymbol{b}) & =\left(M \times N-\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{*} \boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{b}^{*} \boldsymbol{a}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{a} N+\boldsymbol{b} M), \\
(M+\boldsymbol{a}, N+\boldsymbol{b}) & =(M, N)+2(\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b}), \\
\gamma(M+\boldsymbol{a}) & =M-\boldsymbol{a} .
\end{aligned}
$$

(In $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{H})$ the multiplication $M \times N$ and the inner product $(M, N)$ are analogously defined as in $\mathfrak{J}$, and in $\boldsymbol{H}^{3}$ the inner product $(\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b})$ is defined naturally by $\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{*} \boldsymbol{b}+\right.$ $\left.\left.\boldsymbol{b}^{*} \boldsymbol{a}\right)\right)$. Since these operations correspond to their respective operations in $\mathfrak{J}$, hereafter, we identify $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{H}) \oplus \boldsymbol{H}^{3}$ with $\mathfrak{J}$, that is,

$$
\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{H}) \oplus \boldsymbol{H}^{3}=\mathfrak{J} .
$$

The group $F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}}$ is defined to be the automorphism group of the Jordan algebra $\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}=\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{H})$ (in which multiplications ∘ and × are analogously defined as in $\mathfrak{J}$ ):

$$
\begin{aligned}
F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}\right) \mid \alpha(M \circ N)=\alpha M \circ \alpha N\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}\right) \mid \alpha(M \times N)=\alpha M \times \alpha N\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Proposition 2.11.1. $\quad F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}} \cong \operatorname{Sp}(3) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}, \quad \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}=\{E,-E\}$.
Proof. We define a mapping $\varphi: \operatorname{Sp}(3) \rightarrow F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}}$ by

$$
\varphi(A) M=A M A^{*}, \quad M \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}} .
$$

It is not difficult to see that $\varphi$ is well-defined and is a homomorphism. We shall show that $\varphi$ is onto. For a given $\alpha \in F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}}$, we consider $\alpha E_{i}, i=1,2,3$. Since $\alpha E_{i}$ satisfies $\alpha E_{i} \circ \alpha E_{i}=\alpha E_{i}$ and $\operatorname{tr}\left(\alpha E_{i}\right)=1$ (that is, $\alpha E_{i} \in \boldsymbol{H} P_{2}=\left\{M \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}} \mid M \circ M=\right.$
$M, \operatorname{tr}(M)=1\}$ (the quaternion projective plane)), there exists $A_{i} \in \operatorname{Sp}(3)$ such that $\alpha E_{i}=A_{i} E_{i} A_{i}{ }^{*}$. Let $\boldsymbol{a}_{i}=\left(\begin{array}{c}a_{i 1} \\ a_{i 2} \\ a_{i 3}\end{array}\right)$ be the $i$-th column vector of $A_{i}$, then we have $\alpha E_{i}=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}a_{i 1} \bar{a}_{i 1} & a_{i 1} \bar{a}_{i 2} & a_{i 1} \bar{a}_{i 3} \\ a_{i 2} \bar{a}_{i 1} & a_{i 2} \bar{a}_{i 2} & a_{i 2} \bar{a}_{i 3} \\ a_{i 3} \bar{a}_{i 1} & a_{i 3} \bar{a}_{i 2} & a_{i 3} \bar{a}_{i 3}\end{array}\right)$. Construct a matrix $A=\left(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}, \boldsymbol{a}_{2}, \boldsymbol{a}_{3}\right)$, then we have

$$
\alpha E_{i}=A E_{i} A^{*}, \quad i=1,2,3 .
$$

Since $A A^{*}=A\left(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}\right) A^{*}=\alpha E_{1}+\alpha E_{2}+\alpha E_{3}=\alpha E=E$, we have $A \in \operatorname{Sp}(3)$. If we let $\beta=\varphi(A)^{-1} \alpha$, then $\beta \in F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}}$ and satisfies

$$
\beta E_{i}=E_{i}, \quad i=1,2,3 .
$$

Analogously as in Theorem 2.7.1, $\beta$ induces orthogonal transformations $\beta_{1}, \beta_{2}, \beta_{3}$ $: \boldsymbol{H} \rightarrow \boldsymbol{H}$ satisfying

$$
\left(\beta_{1} m\right)\left(\beta_{2} n\right)=\overline{\beta_{3}(\overline{m n})}, \quad m, n \in \boldsymbol{H} .
$$

Put $p=\beta_{1} 1$ and $q=\beta_{2} 1$, then we have $|p|=|q|=1$. Letting $m=1$ and $n=1$ in (i), we have $p\left(\beta_{2} n\right)=\overline{\beta_{3} \bar{n}}$ and $\left(\beta_{1} m\right) q=\overline{\beta_{3} \bar{m}}$, and hence

$$
\beta_{2} m=\bar{p}\left(\beta_{1} m\right) q, \quad \beta_{3} m=\overline{\left(\beta_{1} \bar{m}\right)} q .
$$

Substitute $\zeta m=\bar{p}\left(\beta_{1} m\right)$ in (i), then we see that $\zeta$ satisfies

$$
(\zeta m)(\zeta n)=\zeta(m n), \quad m, n \in \boldsymbol{H},
$$

that is, $\zeta$ is an automorphism of $\boldsymbol{H}: \zeta \in \operatorname{Aut}(\boldsymbol{H})$. Hence, $\zeta$ is expressed by $\zeta m=r m \bar{r}$, using $r \in \operatorname{Sp}(1)$ (Proposition 108 of Yokota [58]). Consequently

$$
\beta_{1} m=\operatorname{prm} \bar{r}, \quad \beta_{2} m=r m \bar{r} q, \quad \beta_{3} m=\bar{q} r m \bar{r} \bar{p}, \quad m \in \boldsymbol{H} .
$$

Construct a matrix $B=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}\bar{q} r & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & p r & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & r\end{array}\right)$, then $B \in \operatorname{Sp}(3)$ and we have

$$
\beta M=B M B^{*}, \quad M \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}},
$$

that is, $\beta=\varphi(B)$. Hence

$$
\alpha=\varphi(A) \beta=\varphi(A) \varphi(B)=\varphi(A B), \quad A B \in \operatorname{Sp}(3) .
$$

Therefore $\varphi$ is onto. $\operatorname{Ker} \varphi=\{E,-E\}$ can be easily obtained. Thus we have the isomorphism $\operatorname{Sp}(3) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \cong F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}}$.

Theorem 2.11.2. $\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma} \cong(\operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}, \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}=\{(1, E),(-1,-E)\}$.

Proof. We define a mapping $\varphi: \operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(3) \rightarrow\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$ by

$$
\varphi(p, A)(M+\boldsymbol{a})=A M A^{*}+p \boldsymbol{a} A^{*}, \quad M+\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}} \oplus \boldsymbol{H}^{3}=\mathfrak{J} .
$$

We first show that $\varphi(p, A) \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$. For $p \in S p(1), A \in S p(3), M, N \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}$ and $\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b} \in \boldsymbol{H}^{3}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
A M A^{*} \times A N A^{*} & =A(M \times N) A^{*} \\
\left(p \boldsymbol{a} A^{*}\right)^{*}\left(p \boldsymbol{b} A^{*}\right) & =A \boldsymbol{a}^{*} \bar{p} p \boldsymbol{b} A^{*}=A\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{*} \boldsymbol{b}\right) A^{*} \\
\left(p \boldsymbol{a} A^{*}\right)\left(A N A^{*}\right) & =p(\boldsymbol{a} N) A^{*}, \text { etc. }
\end{aligned}
$$

From which, we see that $\varphi(p, A)$ satisfies

$$
\varphi(p, A)((M+\boldsymbol{a}) \times(N+\boldsymbol{b}))=\varphi(p, A)(M+\boldsymbol{a}) \times \varphi(p, A)(N+\boldsymbol{b}),
$$

hence $\varphi(p, A) \in F_{4}$. Clearly $\gamma \varphi(p, A)=\varphi(p, A) \gamma$, so that $\varphi(p, A) \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$. Certainly $\varphi$ is a homomorphism. We shall show that $\varphi$ is onto. Let $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$. Since the restriction $\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha \mid \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}$ of $\alpha$ to $\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}=\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid \gamma X=X\}$ belongs to $F_{4, \boldsymbol{H}}$, there exists $A \in \operatorname{Sp}(3)$ such that

$$
\alpha M=A M A^{*}, \quad M \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}
$$

(Proposition 2.11.1). Let $\beta=\varphi(1, A)^{-1} \alpha$, then $\beta \mid \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{H}}=1$. Therefore $\beta \in G_{2}$. Certainly, since $\beta \in \operatorname{Spin}(7)$, by letting $\beta=\left(\beta_{1}, \beta, \kappa \beta\right)$, we see that $\left(\beta_{1} x\right)(\beta y)=$ $\beta(x y)$. Now let $y=1$, then we see $\beta_{1}=\beta$. Since $\beta \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{\gamma}$ and $\beta \mid \boldsymbol{H}=1$, there exists $p \in \operatorname{Sp}(1)$ such that

$$
\beta\left(m+a e_{4}\right)=m+(p a) e_{4}, \quad m+a e_{4} \in \boldsymbol{H} \oplus \boldsymbol{H} e_{4}=\mathfrak{C},
$$

(Theorem 1.10.1). Hence we have

$$
\beta(M+\boldsymbol{a})=M+p \boldsymbol{a}=\varphi(p, E)(M+\boldsymbol{a}),
$$

so that $\beta=\varphi(p, E)$. Hence we have $\alpha=\varphi(1, A) \beta=\varphi(1, A) \varphi(p, E)=\varphi(p, A)$. Therefore $\varphi$ is onto. $\operatorname{Ker} \varphi=\{(1, E),(-1,-E)\}=\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ can be easily obtained. Thus we have the isomorphism $(S p(1) \times S p(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \cong\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$.

Remark. Since $\left(F_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$ is connected as fixed points subgroup of $F_{4}$ by the involution $\gamma$ of the simply connected Lie group $F_{4}$, the fact that $\varphi: \operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(3) \rightarrow F_{4}$ is onto can be proved as follows. The elements

$$
\begin{aligned}
& G_{i j}, \quad 0 \leq i<j \leq 3,4 \leq i<j \leq 7, \\
& \widetilde{A}_{k}\left(e_{j}\right), \quad 0 \leq j \leq 3, k=1,2,3
\end{aligned}
$$

forms an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\left(\mathfrak{f}_{4}\right)^{\gamma}$. So $\operatorname{dim}\left(\left(\mathfrak{f}_{4}\right)^{\gamma}\right)=6 \times 2+4 \times 3=24=3+21=\operatorname{dim}(\mathfrak{s p}(1) \oplus$ $\mathfrak{s} \mathfrak{p}(3))$. Hence $\varphi$ is onto.
2.12. Automorphism $w$ of order 3 and subgroup $(S U(3) \times S U(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}$ of $F_{4}$

We define an $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear transformation $w$ of $\mathfrak{J}$ by

$$
w\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & \omega x_{3} & \overline{\omega x_{2}} \\
\overline{\omega x_{3}} & \xi_{2} & \omega x_{1} \\
\omega x_{2} & \overline{\omega x_{1}} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

This $w$ is the same as $w \in G_{2} \subset F_{4}$.
We shall study the following subgroup $\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$ of $F_{4}$ :

$$
\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}=\left\{\alpha \in F_{4} \mid w \alpha=\alpha w\right\} .
$$

We associate an element

$$
\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right), \quad \xi_{i} \in \boldsymbol{R}, x_{i}=a_{i}+\boldsymbol{m}_{i} \in \boldsymbol{C} \oplus \boldsymbol{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{C}
$$

of $\mathfrak{J}$ with the element

$$
\left(\begin{array}{lll}
\xi_{1} & a_{3} & \bar{a}_{2} \\
\bar{a}_{3} & \xi_{2} & a_{1} \\
a_{2} & \bar{a}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)+\left(\boldsymbol{m}_{1}, \boldsymbol{m}_{2}, \boldsymbol{m}_{3}\right)
$$

of $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$. In $M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, we define the exterior product $M \times N$ by

$$
M \times N=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\boldsymbol{m}_{2} \times \boldsymbol{n}_{3} & \boldsymbol{m}_{3} \times \boldsymbol{n}_{1} & \boldsymbol{m}_{1} \times \boldsymbol{n}_{2} \\
+ & + & + \\
\boldsymbol{n}_{2} \times \boldsymbol{m}_{3} & \boldsymbol{n}_{3} \times \boldsymbol{m}_{1} & \boldsymbol{n}_{1} \times \boldsymbol{m}_{2}
\end{array}\right) \in M(3, C),
$$

where $M=\left(\boldsymbol{m}_{1}, \boldsymbol{m}_{2}, \boldsymbol{m}_{3}\right), N=\left(\boldsymbol{n}_{1}, \boldsymbol{n}_{2}, \boldsymbol{n}_{3}\right) \in M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$. Then, for $P, A \in M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$ and $M, N \in M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, we have

$$
P M \times P N={ }^{t} \widetilde{P}(M \times N), \quad M A \times N A=(M \times N)^{t} \widetilde{A},
$$

where $\widetilde{P}$ and $\widetilde{A}$ are the adjoint matrices of $P$ and $A$, respectively. Further, in $M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, we define a real valued symmetric product $(M, N)$ by

$$
(M, N)=\frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}\left(M^{*} N+N^{*} M\right)=\sum_{i, j}\left(m_{i j}, n_{i j}\right),
$$

where $M=\left(m_{i j}\right), N=\left(n_{i j}\right) \in M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$.
In $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, we define the multiplication × , the inner product ( , ) and the $\boldsymbol{R}$-linear transformation $w$ respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
(X+M) \times(Y+N) & =\left(X \times Y-\frac{1}{2}\left(M^{*} N+N^{*} M\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2}(M Y+N X+\overline{M \times N}), \\
(X+M, Y+N) & =(X, Y)+2(M, N), \\
w(X+M) & =X+\omega_{1} M, \quad\left(\omega_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} e_{1}\right),
\end{aligned}
$$

(in $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, multiplications ○ and × are defined as in $\mathfrak{J}$ ). Since these operations correspond to their respective operations in $\mathfrak{J}$, we identify $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$ with $\mathfrak{J}$, that is,

$$
\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})=\mathfrak{J}
$$

The group $F_{4, \boldsymbol{C}}$ is defined to be the automorphism group of the Jordan algebra $\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{C}}=\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}):$

$$
\begin{aligned}
F_{4, \boldsymbol{C}} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{C}}\right) \mid \alpha(X \circ Y)=\alpha X \circ \alpha Y\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{C}}\right) \mid \alpha(X \times Y)=\alpha X \times \alpha Y\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

The group $\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}=\{1, \epsilon\}$ acts on the group $S U(3)$ by

$$
\epsilon A=\bar{A}, \quad A \in S U(3),
$$

and the group $S U(3) \cdot \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ be the semi-direct product of groups $S U(3)$ and $\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ under this action.

Proposition 2.12.1. $F_{4, \boldsymbol{C}} \cong\left(S U(3) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}\right) \cdot \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}, \quad \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}=\left\{E, \omega_{1} E, \omega_{1}{ }^{2} E\right\}, \omega_{1}=$ $-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} e_{1}$.

Proof. We define a mapping $\varphi: S U(3) \cdot \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \rightarrow F_{4, \boldsymbol{C}}$ by

$$
\varphi(A, 1) X=A X A^{*}, \quad \varphi(A, \epsilon) X=A \bar{X} A^{*}, \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{C}} .
$$

It is not difficult to see that $\varphi$ is well-defined and that it is a homomorphism. The proof that $\varphi$ is onto is the same as that of Proposition 2.11.1 (use $C, S U(3)$, instead of $\boldsymbol{H}, \operatorname{Sp}(3))$, and we only need to modify a little the last part. Since $\zeta \in \operatorname{Aut}(\boldsymbol{C})$ can be either $\zeta=1$ or $\zeta=\epsilon$ (where $\epsilon x=\bar{x}, x \in \boldsymbol{C}$ ), we have

$$
\beta_{1} x=p x, \beta_{2} x=x q, \beta_{3} x=\bar{q} x \bar{p} \quad \text { or } \quad \beta_{1} x=p \bar{x}, \beta_{2} x=\bar{x} q, \beta_{3} x=\bar{q} \bar{x} \bar{p} .
$$

Now, choose $r \in \boldsymbol{C}$ such that $r^{-3}=\bar{q} p$ and construct a matrix $B=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}\bar{q} r & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & p r & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & r\end{array}\right)$. Then, $B \in S U(3)$ and we have

$$
\beta X=B X B^{*} \quad \text { or } \quad \beta X=B \bar{X} B^{*}, \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}_{C} .
$$

The remaining proof is again analogous to that of Proposition 2.11.1.
Theorem 2.12.2. $\left(F_{4}\right)^{w} \cong(S U(3) \times S U(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}, \quad \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}=\left\{(E, E),\left(\omega_{1} E, \omega_{1} E\right)\right.$, $\left.\left(\omega_{1}{ }^{2} E, \omega_{1}{ }^{2} E\right)\right\}, \omega_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} e_{1}$.

Proof. We define a mapping $\varphi: S U(3) \times S U(3) \rightarrow\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$ by

$$
\varphi(P, A)(X+M)=A X A^{*}+P M A^{*}, \quad X+M \in \mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})=\mathfrak{J} .
$$

We first show that $\varphi(P, A) \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$. For $P, A \in S U(3)$ and $X+M, Y+N \in$ $\mathfrak{J}(3, \boldsymbol{C}) \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& A X A^{*} \times A Y A^{*}=A(X \times Y) A^{*} \\
& \left(P M A^{*}\right)^{*}\left(P N A^{*}\right)=A M^{*} P^{*} P N A=A\left(M^{*} N\right) A, \\
& \left(P M A^{*}\right)\left(A Y A^{*}\right)=P(M Y) A^{*}, \\
& \overline{P M A^{*} \times P N A^{*}}=\frac{\bar{t} \widetilde{P}(M \times N)^{t} \widetilde{A} *}{}=P \overline{M \times N} A^{*}, \text { etc. }
\end{aligned}
$$

Using them, we see that $\varphi(P, A)$ satisfies

$$
\varphi(P, A)((X+M) \times(Y+N))=\varphi(P, A)(X+M) \times \varphi(P, A)(Y+N),
$$

hence $\varphi(P, A) \in F_{4}$. Clearly $w \varphi(P, A)=\varphi(P, A) w$, so that $\varphi(P, A) \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$. Certainly $\varphi$ is a homomorphism. We shall show that $\varphi$ is onto. Let $\alpha \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$. Since the restriction $\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha \mid \mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{C}}$ of $\alpha$ to $\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{C}}=\{X \in \mathfrak{J} \mid w X=X\}$ belongs to $F_{4, \boldsymbol{C}}$, there exists $A \in S U(3)$ such that

$$
\alpha X=A X A^{*} \quad \text { or } \quad \alpha X=A \bar{X} A^{*}, \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}_{C}
$$

(Proposition 2.12.1). In the former case, let $\beta=\varphi(E, A)^{-1} \alpha$, then $\beta \mid \mathfrak{J}_{C}=1$, and so $\beta \in G_{2}$. Moreover $\beta \in\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}=\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}=S U(3)$ (Theorem 1.9.4), and hence, there exists $P \in S U(3)$ such that

$$
\beta(X+M)=X+P M=\varphi(P, E)(X+M), \quad X+M \in \mathfrak{J}_{C} \oplus M(3, \boldsymbol{C})=\mathfrak{J} .
$$

Therefore we have

$$
\alpha=\varphi(E, A) \beta=\varphi(E, A) \varphi(P, E)=\varphi(P, A) .
$$

In the latter case, consider the mapping $\gamma_{1}: \mathfrak{J} \rightarrow \mathfrak{J}$ given by $\gamma_{1}(X+M)=\bar{X}+\bar{M}$, $X+M \in \mathfrak{J}$ and remember that $\gamma_{1} \in G_{2} \subset F_{4}$. Let $\beta=\alpha^{-1} \varphi(E, A) \gamma_{1}$. Then $\beta \in F_{4}$ and $\beta \mid \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\boldsymbol{C}}=1$, which shows that $\beta \in\left(G_{2}\right)_{e_{1}}=\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$ (Theorem 1.9.4) $\subset\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$. Since $\alpha, \varphi(E, A) \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$, we have $\gamma_{1} \in\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$, so that $\gamma_{1} \in\left(G_{2}\right)^{w}$ which is a contradiction (Theorem 1.9.4). Consequently the proof of $\varphi$ is onto is completed. The fact that $\operatorname{Ker} \varphi=\left\{(E, E),\left(\omega_{1} E, \omega_{1} E\right),\left(\omega_{1}{ }^{2} E, \omega_{1}{ }^{2} E\right)\right\}=\boldsymbol{Z}_{3}$ can be easily obtained. Thus we have the isomorphism $(S U(3) \times S U(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{3} \cong\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$.

Remark 1. Since $\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$ is connected as the fixed points subgroup of $F_{4}$ by an automorphism of order 3 of the simply connected group $F_{4}$ (Rasevskii [32]), that $\varphi: S U(3) \times S U(3) \rightarrow\left(F_{4}\right)^{w}$ is onto is proved as follows. The elements

$$
\begin{aligned}
& G_{01}, \quad G_{23}, \quad G_{45}, \quad G_{67}, \quad G_{26}+G_{37}, \quad-G_{27}+G_{36}, \\
& G_{24}+G_{35}, \quad-G_{25}+G_{34}, \quad G_{46}+G_{57}, \quad-G_{47}+G_{56}, \\
& \widetilde{A}_{1}(1), \quad \widetilde{A}_{2}(1), \quad \widetilde{A}_{3}(1), \quad \widetilde{A}_{1}\left(e_{1}\right), \quad \widetilde{A}_{2}\left(e_{1}\right), \quad \widetilde{A}_{3}\left(e_{1}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

forms an $\boldsymbol{R}$-basis of $\left(\mathfrak{f}_{4}\right)^{w}$. So $\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathfrak{f}_{4}\right)^{w}=10+6=16=8+8=\operatorname{dim}(\mathfrak{s u}(3)+\mathfrak{s u}(3))$. Hence $\varphi$ is onto.

Remark 2. The group $F_{4}$ has a subgroup which is isomorphic to the group $\left.(S U(3) \times S U(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}\right) \cdot \boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$, which is the semi-direct product of the groups $(S U(3) \times$ $S U(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{3}$ and $\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}$ (the action of $\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}=\left\{1, \gamma_{1}\right\}$ on the group $(S U(3) \times S U(3))$ is $\left.\gamma_{1}(P, A)=(\bar{P}, \bar{A})\right)$.

### 2.13. Complex exceptional Lie group $F_{4}{ }^{C}$

Definition. The group $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is defined to be the automorphism group of the complex Jordan algebra $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
F_{4}^{C} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \alpha(X \circ Y)=\alpha X \circ \alpha Y\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \operatorname{det}(\alpha X)=\operatorname{det} X,(\alpha X, \alpha Y)=(X, Y)\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \operatorname{det}(\alpha X)=\operatorname{det} X, \alpha E=E\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \alpha(X \times Y)=\alpha X \times \alpha Y\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

We define a positive definite Hermitian inner product $\langle X, Y\rangle$ in $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ by

$$
\langle X, Y\rangle=(\tau X, Y) .
$$

For $\alpha \in \operatorname{Hom}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right)$, we denote the complex conjugate transpose of $\alpha$ with respect to $\langle X, Y\rangle$ by $\alpha^{*}:\left\langle\alpha^{*} X, Y\right\rangle=\langle X, \alpha Y\rangle$.

Lemma 2.13.1. (1) For $\alpha \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$, we have $\alpha^{*}=\tau \alpha^{-1} \tau \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$.
(2) For any $\alpha \in F_{4}$, its complexificated mapping $\alpha^{C}: \mathfrak{J}^{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{J}^{C}$ belongs to $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ : $\alpha^{C} \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$. Identifying $\alpha$ with $\alpha^{C}$, we regard $F_{4}$ as a subgroup of $F_{4}{ }^{C}: F_{4} \subset F_{4}{ }^{C}$. For $\alpha \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$, we have $\alpha \in F_{4}$ if and only if $\tau \alpha=\alpha \tau$, that is,

$$
F_{4}=\left\{\alpha \in F_{4}{ }^{C} \mid \tau \alpha=\alpha \tau\right\} .
$$

Proof. (1) $\left\langle\alpha^{*} X, Y\right\rangle=\langle X, \alpha Y\rangle=(\tau X, \alpha Y)=\left(\alpha^{-1} \tau X, Y\right)=\left\langle\tau \alpha^{-1} \tau X, Y\right\rangle$ for all $X, Y \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$. Hence $\alpha^{*}=\tau \alpha^{-1} \tau \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$.
(2) Let $\alpha \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$ satisfy $\tau \alpha=\alpha \tau$. Then, since $\tau \alpha X=\alpha \tau X=\alpha X$, we have $\alpha X \in \mathfrak{J}$ for $X \in \mathfrak{J}$. Hence $\alpha$ induces an $\boldsymbol{R}$-transformation $\alpha^{\prime}$ of $\mathfrak{J}$ and $\alpha^{\prime} \in F_{4}$, further we have $\alpha=\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{C}$.

Theorem 2.13.2. The polar decomposition of the Lie group $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is given by

$$
F_{4}{ }^{C} \simeq F_{4} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{52} .
$$

In particular, $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is a simply connected complex Lie group of type $F_{4}$.

Proof. Evidently $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is an algebraic subgroup of $\operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right)=G L(27, C)$. If $\alpha \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$, then $\alpha^{*} \in F_{4}{ }^{C}$ (Lemma 2.13.1.(1)). Hence from Chevalley's lemma, we have

$$
F_{4}{ }^{C} \simeq\left(F_{4}{ }^{C} \cap U\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right)\right) \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d}=F_{4} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{d},
$$

where $U\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right)=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\}$ and $d=\operatorname{dim} F_{4}{ }^{C}-\operatorname{dim} F_{4}=$ $2 \times 52-52=52$. Since $F_{4}$ is simply connected (Theorem 2.10.2), $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is also simply connected. The Lie algebra of the group $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$, so that $F_{4}{ }^{C}$ is a complex simple Lie group of type $F_{4}$.
2.14. Non-compact exceptional Lie groups $F_{4(4)}$ and $F_{4(-20)}$ of type $F_{4}$

Consider the following two $\boldsymbol{R}$-vector spaces

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right) & =\left\{X \in M\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right) \mid X^{*}=X\right\}, \\
\mathfrak{J}(1,2, \mathfrak{C}) & =\left\{X \in M(3, \mathfrak{C}) \mid I_{1} X^{*} I_{1}=X\right\},
\end{aligned}
$$

where $I_{1}=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,1) \in M(3, \boldsymbol{R})$. We define the Jordan multiplication $X \circ Y$ in $\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right)$ and $\mathfrak{J}(1,2, \mathfrak{C})$ respectively by

$$
X \circ Y=\frac{1}{2}(X Y+Y X)
$$

Then we have the isomorphisms

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right) & \cong\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{C}\right) \mid \tau \gamma X=X\right\}=\left(\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)\right)_{\tau \gamma}, \\
\mathfrak{J}(1,2, \mathfrak{C}) & \cong\left\{X \in \mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{C}\right) \mid \tau \sigma X=X\right\}=\left(\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{C}\right)\right)_{\tau \sigma}
\end{aligned}
$$

as Jordan algebras, therefore we identify them, respectively. We denote by $F_{4(4)}$ and $F_{4(-20)}$ respectively the automorphism groups of the Jordan algebras $\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right)$ and $\mathfrak{J}(1$, $2, \mathfrak{C})$ :

$$
\begin{aligned}
F_{4(4)} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}\left(\mathfrak{J}\left(3, \mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\right)\right) \mid \alpha(X \circ Y)=\alpha X \circ \alpha Y\right\}, \\
F_{4(-20)} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(\mathfrak{J}(1,2, \mathfrak{C})) \mid \alpha(X \circ Y)=\alpha X \circ \alpha Y\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

They can also be defined by

$$
F_{4(4)}=\left(F_{4}{ }^{C}\right)^{\tau \gamma}, \quad F_{4(-20)}=\left(F_{4}{ }^{C}\right)^{\tau \sigma} .
$$

Theorem 2.14.1. The polar decompositions of the Lie groups $F_{4(4)}$ and $F_{4(-20)}$ are respectively given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
F_{4(4)} & \simeq(\operatorname{Sp}(1) \times \operatorname{Sp}(3)) / \boldsymbol{Z}_{2} \times \boldsymbol{R}^{28}, \\
F_{4(-20)} & \simeq \operatorname{Spin}(9) \times \boldsymbol{R}^{16} .
\end{aligned}
$$

Proof. These are facts corresponding to Theorems 2.11.2 and 2.9.1.
Theorem 2.14.2. The centers $z\left(F_{4(4)}\right)$ and $z\left(F_{4(-20)}\right)$ are trivial:

$$
z\left(F_{4(4)}\right)=\{1\}, \quad z\left(F_{4(-20)}\right)=\{1\} .
$$

## Exceptional Lie group $E_{6}$

### 3.1. Compact exceptional Lie group $E_{6}$

Let $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ be the complex exceptional Jordan algebra.

Definition. We define the groups $E_{6}{ }^{C}$ and $E_{6}$ respectively by

$$
\begin{aligned}
E_{6}^{C} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \operatorname{det}(\alpha X)=\operatorname{det} X\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid(\alpha X, \alpha Y, \alpha Z)=(X, Y, Z)\right\}, \\
E_{6} & =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \operatorname{det}(\alpha X)=\operatorname{det} X,\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid(\alpha X, \alpha Y, \alpha Z)=(X, Y, Z),\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \alpha X \times \alpha Y={ }^{t} \alpha^{-1}(X \times Y),\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\} \\
& =\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid \alpha X \times \alpha Y=\tau \alpha \tau(X \times Y),\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\} .
\end{aligned}
$$

If we define an involutive automorphism $\lambda$ of the group $E_{6}{ }^{C}$ by

$$
\lambda(\alpha)={ }^{t} \alpha^{-1}, \quad \alpha \in E_{6}{ }^{C}
$$

(Lemma 2.2.3), then the definition of the group $E_{6}$ can be also given by

$$
E_{6}=\left\{\alpha \in E_{6}{ }^{C} \mid \tau \lambda(\alpha) \tau=\alpha\right\}=\left(E_{6}{ }^{C}\right)^{\tau \lambda} .
$$

Theorem 3.1.1. $E_{6}$ is a compact Lie group.
Proof. $E_{6}$ is a compact Lie group as a closed subgroup of the unitary group

$$
U(27)=U\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right)=\left\{\alpha \in \operatorname{Iso}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid\langle\alpha X, \alpha Y\rangle=\langle X, Y\rangle\right\} .
$$

3.2. Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ of $E_{6}$

Before investigating the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ of the group $E_{6}$, we will study the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ of the group $E_{6}{ }^{C}$.

Theorem 3.2.1. (1) The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ of the Lie group $E_{6}{ }^{C}$ is given by

$$
\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}=\left\{\phi \in \operatorname{Hom}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid(\phi X, X, X)=0\right\} .
$$

(2) Any element $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is uniquely expressed by
$$
\phi=\delta+\widetilde{T}, \quad \delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}^{C}, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}^{C} .
$$
In particular, the dimension of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is
$$
\operatorname{dim}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}\right)=52+26=78 .
$$

Proof. (1) is proved as similar way to Lemma 2.3.1.
(2) For $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}^{C}$, by letting $T=\phi E$, we obtain $T \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, and $\operatorname{tr}(T)=0$. Certainly $\operatorname{tr}(T)=(T, E, E)=(\phi E, E, E)=0$. If we put $\delta=\phi-\widetilde{T}$, then $\delta \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$. Moreover $\delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$, because $\delta E=\phi E-\widetilde{T} E=T-T=0$. Hence we have $\phi=\delta+\widetilde{T}$. To prove the uniqueness of the expression, it is sufficient to show that

$$
\delta+\widetilde{T}=0, \quad \delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C} \quad \text { implies } \quad \delta=0, T=0 .
$$

Certainly, let apply it on $E$, then we have $T=0$, so that $\delta=0$. Finally, we have $\operatorname{dim}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}\right)=52+26=78$ from the expression above. Therefore the theorem is proved.

Theorem 3.2.2. The Lie bracket $\left[\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right]$ in $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is given by

$$
\left[\delta_{1}+\widetilde{T}_{1}, \delta_{2}+\widetilde{T}_{2}\right]=\left(\left[\delta_{1}, \delta_{2}\right]+\left[\widetilde{T}_{1}, \widetilde{T}_{2}\right]\right)+\left(\widetilde{\delta_{1} T_{2}}-\widetilde{\delta_{2} T_{1}}\right),
$$

where $\phi_{i}=\delta_{i}+\widetilde{T}_{i}, \delta_{i} \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$.
Proof. It is sufficient to show that $[\delta, \widetilde{T}]=\widetilde{\delta T}$ for $\delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$. Now,

$$
\begin{aligned}
{[\delta, \widetilde{T}] X } & =\delta(T \circ X)-T \circ \delta X=\delta T \circ X+T \circ \delta X-T \circ \delta X \\
& =\delta T \circ X=\widetilde{\delta T} X, \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}^{C} .
\end{aligned}
$$

We shall investigate the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ of the Lie group $E_{6}$.
Lemma 3.2.3. For $\phi=\delta+\widetilde{T} \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}, \delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
\lambda(\phi)=-{ }^{t} \phi=-{ }^{t}(\delta+\widetilde{T})=\delta-\widetilde{T} .
$$

In particular, $-{ }^{t} \phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$.
Proof. $\left(-{ }^{t} \phi X, Y\right)=-(X, \phi Y)=-(X, \delta Y+\widetilde{T} Y)=-(X, \delta Y)-(X, T \circ Y)$

$$
=(\delta X, Y)-(\widetilde{T} X, Y)=((\delta-\widetilde{T}) X, Y) \quad X, Y \in \mathfrak{J}^{C} .
$$

Therefore $-{ }^{t} \phi=\delta-\widetilde{T} \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$.
Theorem 3.2.4. (1) The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ of the Lie group $E_{6}$ is given by

$$
\mathfrak{e}_{6}=\left\{\phi \in \operatorname{Hom}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right) \mid(\phi X, X, X)=0,\langle\phi X, Y\rangle+\langle X, \phi Y\rangle=0\right\} .
$$

(2) Any element $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}$ is uniquely expressed by

$$
\phi=\delta+i \widetilde{T}, \quad \delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0} .
$$

Proof. (1) The proof is evident (cf. Lemma 2.3.1).
(2) For $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$, the condition $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}$ is equivalent to $\tau \lambda(\phi) \tau=\phi$. Now, if $\phi$ is of the form $\phi=\delta+\widetilde{T^{\prime}}, \delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T^{\prime} \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$ (Theorem 3.2.1.(2)), then $\tau \lambda(\phi) \tau=\phi$ is
$\tau \delta \tau-\widetilde{\tau T^{\prime}}=\delta+\widetilde{T^{\prime}}$ (Lemma 3.2.3), that is, $\tau \delta \tau=\delta$ and $\tau T^{\prime}=-T^{\prime}$. Hence $\delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}$, and $T^{\prime}$ is of the form $T^{\prime}=i T, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}$.

Proposition 3.2.5. The complexification of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ is $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$.
Proof. For $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$, the conjugate transposed mapping $\phi^{*}$ of $\phi$ with respect to the inner product $\langle X, Y\rangle$ of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ is $\phi^{*}=\tau^{t} \phi \tau \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$, and for $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}, \phi$ belongs to $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ if and only if $\phi^{*}=-\phi$. Now, any element $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ can be uniquely expressed as

$$
\phi=\frac{\phi-\phi^{*}}{2}+i \frac{\phi+\phi^{*}}{2 i}, \quad \frac{\phi-\phi^{*}}{2}, \frac{\phi+\phi^{*}}{2 i} \in \mathfrak{e}_{6} .
$$

Hence $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is the complexification of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$.

### 3.3. Simplicity of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$

Theorem 3.3.1. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is simple and so $\mathfrak{e}_{6}$ is also simple.
Proof. We use the decomposition of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ of Theorem 3.2.1.(2):

$$
\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}=\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \oplus \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C} .
$$

Let $p: \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C} \rightarrow \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$ and $q: \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C} \rightarrow \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}{ }^{C}$ be projections of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}=\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \oplus \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}{ }^{C}$. Now, let $\mathfrak{a}$ be a non-zero ideal of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$. Then $p(\mathfrak{a})$ is an ideal of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$. Indeed, if $\delta \in p(\mathfrak{a})$, then there exists $T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$ such that $\delta+\widetilde{T} \in \mathfrak{a}$. For any $\delta_{1} \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
\mathfrak{a} \ni\left[\delta_{1}, \delta+\widetilde{T}\right]=\left[\delta_{1}, \delta\right]+\widetilde{\delta_{1} T} \text { (Theorem 3.2.2), }
$$

hence $\left[\delta_{1}, \delta\right] \in \mathfrak{a}$.
We shall show that either $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$ or $\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$. Assume that $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$ and $\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$. Then the mapping $p \mid \mathfrak{a}: \mathfrak{a} \rightarrow \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$ is injective because $\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$. Since $p(\mathfrak{a})$ is a non-zero ideal of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$ and $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$ is simple, we have $p(\mathfrak{a})=\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$. Hence $\operatorname{dim}_{C} \mathfrak{a}=\operatorname{dim}_{C} p(\mathfrak{a})=\operatorname{dim}_{C} \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}=52$. On the other hand, since $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\{0\}$, $q \mid \mathfrak{a}: \mathfrak{a} \rightarrow \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C}$ is also injective, we have $\operatorname{dim}_{C} \mathfrak{a} \leq \operatorname{dim}_{C} \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C}=\operatorname{dim}_{C} \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}=26$. This leads to a contradiction.

We now consider the following two cases.
(1) Case $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$. From the simplicity of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$, we have $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$, hence $\mathfrak{a} \supset \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$. On the other hand, we have

$$
\mathfrak{a} \supset\left[\mathfrak{a}, \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}\right] \supset\left[\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, \widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}{ }^{C}\right]=\mathfrak{f}_{4} \widetilde{ }^{C} \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C} \text { (Lemma 3.2.2) }=\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C} \text { (Proposition 2.4.6.(2)). }
$$

Consequently $\mathfrak{a} \supset \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \oplus \tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}{ }^{C}=\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$.
(2) Case $\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a} \neq\{0\}$. Let $\widetilde{A}\left(A \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}\right)$ be a non-zero element of $\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a} \subset \mathfrak{a}$. Choose $B \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$ such that $[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}] \neq 0$ (Lemma 2.5.4), then $0 \neq[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}] \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C} \cap \mathfrak{a}$. Hence this case is reduced to the case (1).

Therefore we have $\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$.
Proposition 3.3.2. (1) $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ is a simple Jordan algebra.

(2) $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ is an $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$-irreducible $C$-module.
(3) $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C} \mathfrak{J}^{C}=\left\{\sum_{i} \phi_{i} A_{i} \mid \phi_{i} \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}, A_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}\right\}=\mathfrak{J}^{C}$.

Proof. (1) Let $\mathfrak{a}$ be a non-zero ideal of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ and $X=X(\xi, x)$ a non-zero element of $\mathfrak{a}$.

(i) Case $\xi_{1} \neq 0$. From $\xi_{1} E_{1}=\left(2 X \circ E_{1}-X\right) \circ E_{1} \in \mathfrak{a}$, we have $E_{1} \in \mathfrak{a}$. Next, from $F_{2}(1)=2 E_{1} \circ F_{2}(1) \in \mathfrak{a}$ and $E_{1}+E_{3}=F_{2}(1) \circ F_{2}(1) \in \mathfrak{a}$, we have $E_{3}=\left(E_{1}+E_{3}\right)-E_{1} \in \mathfrak{a}$. Similarly $E_{2} \in \mathfrak{a}$, so that $E=E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3} \in \mathfrak{a}$. Now, for any $X \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}, X=E \circ X \in \mathfrak{a}$, and so $\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{J}^{C}$. In the case $\xi_{2} \neq 0$ or $\xi_{3} \neq 0$, the statement is also valid.
(ii) Case $\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi_{3}=0, x_{1} \neq 0$. We have $F_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)=4\left(X \circ E_{2}\right) \circ E_{3} \in \mathfrak{a}$. Choose $a \in \mathfrak{C}^{C}$ such that $\left(x_{1}, a\right)=1$, then $F_{1}\left(x_{1}\right) \circ F_{1}(a)=\left(x_{1}, a\right)\left(E_{2}+E_{3}\right)=E_{2}+E_{3} \in \mathfrak{a}$. Hence this case is reduced to the case (i) and so $\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{J}^{C}$.
(2) Let $W$ be a non-zero $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$-invariant $C$-submodule of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$. For any $A \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$ and $X \in W$, we have
$$
A \circ X=\left(A-\frac{1}{3} \operatorname{tr}(A) E\right)^{\sim} X+\frac{1}{3} \operatorname{tr}(A) X \in W .
$$
Hence, $W$ is an ideal of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$. From the simplicity of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}\left((1)\right.$ above), we have $W=\mathfrak{J}^{C}$.
(3) $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C} \mathfrak{J}^{C}$ is an $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$-invariant $C$-submodule of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$. Hence from the irreducibility of $\mathfrak{J}^{C}$ ((2) above), we have $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C} \mathfrak{J}^{C}=\mathfrak{J}^{C}$.

3.4. Element $A \vee B$ of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$

Definition. For $A, B \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, we define an element $A \vee B \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ by

$$
A \vee B=[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}]+\left(A \circ B-\frac{1}{3}(A, B) E\right)^{\sim}
$$

(Proposition 2.4.1, Theorem 3.2.1).
Lemma 3.4.1. For $A, B \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, we have

$$
(A \vee B) X=\frac{1}{2}(B, X) A+\frac{1}{6}(A, B) X-2 B \times(A \times X), \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}^{C} .
$$

Proof. Consider Hamilton-Cayley formula $X \circ(X \times X)=(\operatorname{det} X) E, X \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, that is,

$$
X \circ(X \circ X)-\operatorname{tr}(X) X \circ X+\frac{1}{2}\left(\operatorname{tr}(X)^{2}-(X, X)\right) X=\frac{1}{3}(X, X, X) E .
$$

If we put $\lambda A+\mu B+\nu X$ in place of $X$, then taking the coefficient of $\lambda \mu \nu$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
A & \circ(B \circ X)+B \circ(X \circ A)+X \circ(A \circ B)-\operatorname{tr}(A) B \circ X-\operatorname{tr}(B) A \circ X \\
& -\operatorname{tr}(X) A \circ B+\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(B) X+\operatorname{tr}(B) \operatorname{tr}(X) A+\operatorname{tr}(X) \operatorname{tr}(A) B) \\
& -\frac{1}{2}((A, B) X+(B, X) A+(X, A) B)=(A, B, X) E .
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, using the above, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \frac{1}{2}(B, X) A+\frac{1}{6}(A, B) X-2 B \times(A \times X)+\frac{1}{3}(A, B) X \\
= & \frac{1}{2}(B, X) A+\frac{1}{2}(A, B) X-2 B \circ(A \times X)+\operatorname{tr}(B) A \times X+\operatorname{tr}(A \times X) B \\
& -(\operatorname{tr}(B) \operatorname{tr}(A \times X)-(B, A \times X)) E \\
= & \frac{1}{2}(B, X) A+\frac{1}{2}(A, B) X-2 B \circ(A \circ X)+\operatorname{tr}(A) B \circ X+\operatorname{tr}(X) B \circ A \\
& -\operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(X) B+(A, X) B+\operatorname{tr}(B) A \circ X-\frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}(B) \operatorname{tr}(A) X \\
& -\frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}(B) \operatorname{tr}(X) A+\frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}(B) \operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(X) E-\frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}(B)(A, X) E \\
& +\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(X)-(A, X)) B-\frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}(B)(\operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(X)-(A, X)) E+(A, B, X) E \\
= & \operatorname{tr}(A) B \circ X+\operatorname{tr}(B) X \circ A+\operatorname{tr}(X) A \circ B \\
& -\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(B) X+\operatorname{tr}(B) \operatorname{tr}(X) A+\operatorname{tr}(A) \operatorname{tr}(X) B) \\
& +\frac{1}{2}((A, B) X+(B, X) A+(A, X) B)+(A, B, X) E-2 B \circ(A \circ X) \\
= & A \circ(B \circ X)+B \circ(A \circ X)+X \circ(A \circ B)-2 B \circ(A \circ X) \\
= & A \circ(B \circ X)-B \circ(A \circ X)+(A \circ B) \circ X \\
= & {[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}] X+(A \circ B)^{\sim} X . }
\end{aligned}
$$

Lemma 3.4.2. (1) $E_{i} \vee E_{j}=0, i \neq j$.

(2) $E_{1} \vee E_{1}=\frac{1}{3}\left(2 E_{1}-E_{2}-E_{3}\right)^{\sim}$, more explicitly, we have
$$
\left(E_{1} \vee E_{1}\right)\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
\xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & \xi_{2} & x_{1} \\
x_{2} & \bar{x}_{1} & \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{6}\left(\begin{array}{ccc}
4 \xi_{1} & x_{3} & \bar{x}_{2} \\
\bar{x}_{3} & -2 \xi_{2} & -2 x_{1} \\
x_{2} & -2 \bar{x}_{1} & -2 \xi_{3}
\end{array}\right) .
$$

For $\phi \in \operatorname{Hom}_{C}\left(\mathfrak{J}^{C}\right)$, we often denote $-{ }^{t} \phi$ by $\phi^{\prime}$ :

$$
\left(\phi^{\prime} X, Y\right)=-(X, \phi Y), \quad X, Y \in \mathfrak{J}^{C} .
$$

Lemma 3.4.3. (1) For $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
\phi(X \times Y)=\phi^{\prime} X \times Y+X \times \phi^{\prime} Y, \quad X, Y \in \mathfrak{J}^{C} .
$$

(2) For $A, B \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, we have
$$
(A \vee B)^{\prime}=-B \vee A .
$$

Proof. (1) it is evident from Lemma 2.3.1.

(2) $(A \vee B)^{\prime}=\left([\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}]+\left(A \circ B-\frac{1}{3}(A, B) E\right)^{\sim}\right)^{\prime}=[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}]-\left(A \circ B-\frac{1}{3}(A, B) E\right)^{\sim}$ $($ Lemma 3.2.3 $)=-[\widetilde{B}, \widetilde{A}]-\left(A \circ B-\frac{1}{3}(A, B) E\right)^{\sim}=-B \vee A$.

Lemma 3.4.4. (1) For $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ and $A, B \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, we have

$$
[\phi, A \vee B]=\phi A \vee B+A \vee \phi^{\prime} B .
$$

(2) Any element $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is expressed by $\phi=\sum_{i}\left(A_{i} \vee B_{i}\right), A_{i}, B_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$.

Proof. (1) $[\phi, A \vee B] X=\phi(A \vee B) X-(A \vee B) \phi X$

$$
\begin{aligned}
= & \phi\left(\frac{1}{2}(B, X) A+\frac{1}{6}(A, B) X-2 B \times(A \times X)\right)-(A \vee B) \phi X \text { (Lemma 3.4.1) } \\
= & \frac{1}{2}(B, X) \phi A+\frac{1}{6}(A, B) \phi X-2 \phi^{\prime} B \times(A \times X)-2 B \times(\phi A \times X) \\
& -2 B \times(A \times \phi X)-\frac{1}{2}(B, \phi X) A-\frac{1}{6}(A, B) \phi X+2 B \times(A \times \phi X) \text { (Lemma 3.4.3) } \\
= & \frac{1}{2}(B, X) \phi A+\frac{1}{6}(\phi A, B) X-2 B \times(\phi A \times X) \\
& +\frac{1}{2}\left(\phi^{\prime} B, X\right) A+\frac{1}{6}\left(A, \phi^{\prime} B\right) X-2 \phi^{\prime} B \times(A \times X) \\
= & (\phi A \vee B) X+\left(A \vee \phi^{\prime} B\right) X, \quad X \in \mathfrak{J}^{C} .
\end{aligned}
$$

(2) We can see from (1) that $\mathfrak{a}=\left\{\sum_{i}\left(A_{i} \vee B_{i}\right) \mid A_{i}, B_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}\right\}$ is an ideal of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$. From the simplicity of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ (Theorem 3.3.1) we have $\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$.

### 3.5. Killing form of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$

Definition. We define a symmetric inner product $\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right)_{6}$ in $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ by

$$
\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right)_{6}=\left(\delta_{1}, \delta_{2}\right)_{4}+\left(T_{1}, T_{2}\right),
$$

where $\phi_{i}=\delta_{i}+\widetilde{T}_{i}, \delta_{i} \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$.
Lemma 3.5.1. (1) The inner product $\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right)_{6}$ of $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$-adjoint invariant:

$$
\left(\left[\phi, \phi_{1}\right], \phi_{2}\right)_{6}+\left(\phi_{1},\left[\phi, \phi_{2}\right]\right)_{6}=0, \quad \phi, \phi_{i} \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}^{C} .
$$

(2) For $\phi \in \mathfrak{e}_{6}^{C}, A, B \in \mathfrak{J}^{C}$, we have
$$
(\phi, A \vee B)_{6}=(\phi A, B) .
$$

Proof. (1) For $\phi=\delta+\widetilde{T}, \phi_{i}=\delta_{i}+\widetilde{T}_{i}, \delta, \delta_{i} \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T, T_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \left(\left[\phi, \phi_{1}\right], \phi_{2}\right)_{6} \\
& =\left(\left[\delta+\widetilde{T}, \delta_{1}+\widetilde{T}_{1}\right], \delta_{2}+\widetilde{T}_{2}\right)_{6}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& =\left(\left(\left[\delta, \delta_{1}\right]+\left[\widetilde{T}, \widetilde{T}_{1}\right]\right)+\left(\widetilde{\delta T_{1}}-\widetilde{\delta_{1} T}\right), \delta_{2}+\widetilde{T}_{2}\right)_{6} \quad(\text { Theorem 3.2.2 }) \\
& =\left(\left[\delta, \delta_{1}\right], \delta_{2}\right)_{4}+\left(\left[\widetilde{T}, \widetilde{T}_{1}\right], \delta_{2}\right)_{4}+\left(\delta T_{1}-\delta_{1} T, T_{2}\right) \\
& =-\left(\delta_{1},\left[\delta, \delta_{2}\right]\right)_{4}+\left(\delta_{2} T, T_{1}\right)+\left(\delta T_{1}, T_{2}\right)-\left(\delta_{1} T, T_{2}\right) \quad(\text { Lemma } 2.5 .2) \\
& =-\left(\delta_{1},\left[\delta, \delta_{2}\right]\right)_{4}-\left(\delta_{1} T, T_{2}\right)-\left(T_{1}, \delta T_{2}\right)+\left(T_{1}, \delta_{2} T\right) \\
& \left.=-\left(\delta_{1}+\widetilde{T}_{1},\left[\delta, \delta_{2}\right]+\left[\widetilde{T}, \widetilde{T}_{2}\right]\right)+\left(\widetilde{\delta T_{2}}-\widetilde{\delta_{2} T}\right)\right)_{6} \\
& =-\left(\delta_{1}+\widetilde{T}_{1},\left[\delta+\widetilde{T}, \delta_{2}+\widetilde{T}_{2}\right]\right)_{6} \\
& =-\left(\phi_{1},\left[\phi, \phi_{2}\right]\right)_{6}
\end{aligned}
$$

(2) For $\phi=\delta+\widetilde{T}, \delta \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\phi, A \vee B)_{6} & =\left(\delta+\widetilde{T},[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}]+\left(A \circ B-\frac{1}{3}(A, B) E\right)^{\sim}\right)_{6} \\
& =(\delta,[\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B}])_{4}+\left(T, A \circ B-\frac{1}{3}(A, B) E\right) \\
& =(\delta A, B)+(\widetilde{T} A, B)=((\delta+\widetilde{T}) A, B)=(\phi A, B) .
\end{aligned}
$$

Lemma 3.5.2. In $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$, we have

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
{\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, D\right]=0, D \in \mathfrak{d}_{4}^{C},} & {\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim},\left(E_{j}-E_{j+1}\right)^{\sim}\right]=0,} \\
{\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, \widetilde{A}_{i}(a)\right]=-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{F}_{i}(a),} & {\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, \widetilde{F}_{i}(a)\right]=-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{A}_{i}(a),} \\
{\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, \widetilde{A}_{i+1}(a)\right]=-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{F}_{i+1}(a),} & {\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, \widetilde{F}_{i+1}(a)\right]=-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{A}_{i+1}(a),} \\
{\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, \widetilde{A}_{i+2}(a)\right]=\widetilde{F}_{i+2}(a),} & {\left[\left(E_{i}-E_{i+1}\right)^{\sim}, \widetilde{F}_{i+2}(a)\right]=\widetilde{A}_{i+2}(a) .}
\end{array}
$$

Theorem 3.5.3. The Killing form $B_{6}$ of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
B_{6}\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right) & =12\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right)_{6} \\
& =12\left(\delta_{1}, \delta_{2}\right)_{4}+12\left(T_{1}, T_{2}\right) \\
& =\frac{4}{3} B_{4}\left(\delta_{1}, \delta_{2}\right)+12\left(T_{1}, T_{2}\right) \\
& =4 \operatorname{tr}\left(\phi_{1} \phi_{2}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\phi_{i}=\delta_{i}+\widetilde{T}_{i}, \delta_{i} \in \mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}, T_{i} \in \mathfrak{J}_{0}{ }^{C}$ and $B_{4}$ is the Killing form of $\mathfrak{f}_{4}{ }^{C}$.
Proof. Since $\mathfrak{e}_{6}{ }^{C}$ is simple (Theorem 3.3.1), there exist $k, k^{\prime} \in C$ such that

$$
B_{6}\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right)=k\left(\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}\right)_{6}=k^{\prime} \operatorname{tr}\left(\phi_{1} \phi_{2}\right) .
$$

To determine these $k, k^{\prime}$, let $\phi=\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=\left(E_{1}-E_{2}\right)^{\sim}$. Then we have

$$
(\phi, \phi)_{6}=\left(\left(E_{1}-E_{2}\right)^{\sim},\left(E_{1}-E_{2}\right)^{\sim}\right)_{6}=\left(E_{1}-E_{2}, E_{1}-E_{2}\right)=2 .
$$

On the other hand, $(\operatorname{ad} \phi)^{2}$ is calculated as follows.

$$
\left[\phi,\left[\phi, \widetilde{A}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]\right]=\left[\phi,-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{F}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{4} \widetilde{A}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right),
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& {\left[\phi,\left[\phi, \widetilde{A}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]\right]=\left[\phi,-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{F}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{4} \widetilde{A}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right),} \\
& {\left[\phi,\left[\phi, \widetilde{A}_{3}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]\right]=\left[\phi, \widetilde{F}_{3}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]=\widetilde{A}_{3}\left(e_{i}\right),} \\
& {\left[\phi,\left[\phi, \widetilde{F}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]\right]=\left[\phi,-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{A}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{4} \widetilde{F}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right),} \\
& {\left[\phi,\left[\phi, \widetilde{F}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]\right]=\left[\phi,-\frac{1}{2} \widetilde{A}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{4} \widetilde{F}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right),} \\
& {\left[\phi,\left[\phi, \widetilde{F}_{3}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]\right]=\left[\phi, \widetilde{A}_{3}\left(e_{i}\right)\right]=\widetilde{F}_{3}\left(e_{i}\right),}
\end{aligned}
$$

the others $=0$.
Hence

$$
B_{6}(\phi, \phi)=\operatorname{tr}\left((\operatorname{ad} \phi)^{2}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{4} \times 4+1 \times 2\right) \times 8=24 .
$$

Therefore $k=12$. Next, we will calculate $\operatorname{tr}(\phi \phi)$ as follows.

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
\phi \phi E_{1}=\phi E_{1}=E_{1}, & \phi \phi \widetilde{F}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)=-\frac{1}{2} \phi \widetilde{F}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{4} \widetilde{F}_{1}\left(e_{i}\right), \\
\phi \phi E_{2}=-\phi E_{2}=E_{2}, & \phi \phi \widetilde{F}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{2} \phi \widetilde{F}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{4} \widetilde{F}_{2}\left(e_{i}\right), \\
